        The conflict arose because:
            •	The Dutch republic of Transvaal refused to grant political rights to Anglo-Saxon immigrants.
            •	Other Boer republics also denied these rights.
        In Britain, public opinion saw this as a just war. The Boers were denying lawful rights to British citizens.
            However, European and global opinion viewed it as British imperialism.

    The Boer War
        At the start of the 20th century, Britain and the Boers clashed over South Africa. 
            The Boers, having previously defeated Britain, were aware of its military weaknesses in the region. 
            They believed that before the war ended, some European power would intervene on their behalf.
        British Underestimation
            The British underestimated:
                •	The tenacity and tactical skill of Boer fighters.
                •	The advantage of fighting on home ground.
            The Dutch-class irregulars were strong fighters but suffered from:
                •	Lax discipline
                •	Excessive caution
                •	Lack of unified strategy
            Had they overcome these weaknesses, the war could have lasted longer in their favor.
            Course of the War
                •	Several small battles took place.
                •	Boer forces used thin, extended defensive lines, making them difficult to penetrate.
                •	The British gradually gained successes.
            The war spanned thousands of square miles and involved 450,000 British soldiers. It ended on 31 May 1902, after a two-and-a-half-year campaign.
            The British secured victory by:
                •	Splitting the war zone into smaller resistance areas.
                •	Using mounted columns to strike at Boer mobility.
            Impact of the War
                Inside Britain:
                    •	The Liberals came to power in 1905.
                    •	The new Secretary of State for War, R.B. Haldane, took key steps:
                    •	Maintained British naval supremacy.
                    •	Reorganized the army, drawing lessons from the Boer War.
                Outside Britain:
                    •	The war showcased British imperial unity.
                    •	Britain’s military reputation suffered due to prolonged Boer resistance.
                    •	However, Britain’s naval power prevented European rivals from aiding the Boers.

    - Politics of Alliances
        At the beginning of the 20th century, Britain’s long-standing policy of splendid isolation was becoming impractical. 
            Maintaining naval supremacy alone was no longer sufficient, and Britain began seeking an ally on the European mainland.
        3.1. Germany as a Potential Ally
            Britain initially considered Germany due to:
                •	Its military strength, as the most formidable power in Europe.
                •	Its rising population and tradition of aggressive diplomacy.
                •	Its potential as a counterweight to Russia, Britain’s key rival in the Middle and Far East.
                •	Its historical ties with Britain:
                    •	The Kaiser of Germany was Queen Victoria’s grandson.
                    •	Germany had helped Wellington at Waterloo.
            British Efforts to Secure an Alliance
                •	During the Boer War, British Colonial Secretary Joseph Chamberlain sought closer ties with Germany.
                •	He proposed a secret agreement over Morocco, where Germany’s influence was growing.
            German Resistance
                •	Baron von Holstein, a German Foreign Ministry official, advised the Kaiser against an alliance.
                •	He argued that Britain always used others for its own benefit.
                •	He encouraged Germany to demand a higher price for its friendship.
                •	Holstein believed a British-French alliance was unlikely, making Britain dependent on Germany.
        3.2. Shift Towards France
            Britain, frustrated by Germany’s reluctance, began reconsidering its alliances.
            Factors Leading to a French Alliance
                •	The British press urged an understanding with Russia, even at the cost of concessions in China and the Persian Gulf.
                •	Growing concerns over Germany’s expanding naval power pushed Britain to seek alternatives.
                •	France, though a traditional enemy, became a potential partner.
            Challenges in a British-French Alliance
                •	Britain was aristocratic, whereas France was the only acknowledged republic in Europe.
                •	France had suffered British indifference when defeated by Germany thirty years earlier.
                •	The two nations had nearly gone to war in 1898 over Fashoda (later Kodok) in Sudan.
                •	France’s internal instability:
                    •	It was seen as divided, with a weak government.
                    •	The appointment of Alexander Millerand, a Socialist, to the French Cabinet in 1899 worried Britain’s Conservative government.

    - The Anglo-French Entente
        Despite obstacles, Anglo-French relations improved at the start of the 20th century.
        4.1. The Role of King Edward VII
            •	May 1903: King Edward VII visited Paris, breaking the diplomatic deadlock.
            •	His visit paved the way for negotiations between:
                •	Lansdowne (British Foreign Minister)
                •	Delcassé (French Foreign Minister)
        4.2. Formation of the Entente (April 1904)
            The negotiations resulted in a series of agreements known as the Anglo-French Entente, the most significant diplomatic event of the early 20th century.
                It remained in effect until France fell in World War II.
        4.3. Key Agreements
            4.3.1. First Agreement (Egypt & Morocco)
                •	Britain agreed not to alter Egypt’s political status.
                    •	Egypt remained a semi-independent Turkish province but was under British occupation.
                    •	France would not interfere in British affairs there.
                •	France agreed not to alter Morocco’s political status.
                    •	Morocco was under French influence.
                    •	Britain would not interfere in French affairs there.
                •	Additional terms:
                    •	Trade freedom in Egypt and Morocco for 30 years.
                    •	No fortifications on the Moorish coast opposite Gibraltar.
                    •	France would negotiate separately with Spain on Morocco’s status.
                    •	The Egyptian government could manage its finances, provided it paid its debt interest.
                    •	Suez Canal matters were settled as per French wishes.
            4.3.2. Second Agreement (Newfoundland & West Africa)
                •	France gave up its fishing rights off Newfoundland’s coast.
                •	In return, France gained uninterrupted access between its territories on:
                    •	The Niger River.
                    •	Lake Chad (West Africa).
            4.3.3. Third Agreement (Southeast Asia & Madagascar)
                •	Both nations agreed to:
                    •	No military intervention or special privileges in the Menam Basin (Thailand).
                •	France recognized British influence:
                    •	West of the neutral zone (Siamese territory).
                    •	Gulf of Siam, Malaya Peninsula, and adjacent islands.
                •	Britain recognized French influence:
                    •	East and southeast of the neutral zone.
                •	Britain withdrew objections to:
                    •	French tariffs in Madagascar (occupied by France in 1896).
                •	1906: Britain and France established a joint rule (condominium) over the New Hebrides islands.
        4.4. Impact of the Anglo-French Entente
            •	The agreement resolved many outstanding disputes between Britain and France.
            •	It strengthened their diplomatic ties, paving the way for future cooperation.
            •	However, it also deepened European divisions, leading to the formation of two opposing military blocs.
            •	Lord Rosebery (British ex-Premier) noted:
                •	Germany, Europe’s strongest military power, was excluded from the negotiations.
                •	The neglect of Germany’s interests in Morocco contributed to growing tensions.
                •	Ultimately, this pushed Britain towards war against Germany.

    - The Franco-Russian Alliance
        The Anglo-French Entente influenced Russo-British relations, which had been hostile for most of the 19th century.
        5.1. Russia’s Shift in Focus
            •	After bitter experiences in Bulgaria, the Russian Tsars turned their attention towards the Far East.
            •	The collapsing Chinese Empire presented new expansion opportunities for Russia.
            •	To secure Western support, Russia sought an alliance with France.
        5.2. Strengthening Franco-Russian Ties
            •	Since the late 1880s, relations between France and Russia improved due to:
                •	Financial transactions.
                •	Military exchanges.
            •	1891: A political agreement was reached to coordinate foreign policy.
            •	1895: A military convention was officially announced.
                •	The alliance aimed to counter any attack by the Triple Alliance (Germany, Austria-Hungary, Italy).
        5.3. Reaction from Other Powers
            •	The Franco-Russian Alliance was met with suspicion and disapproval in Berlin and London.
            •	However, the alliance did not mask the military and political weaknesses of both partners.
            •	Historians note:
                •	Both France and Russia were weaker than they appeared.
                •	Over the next ten years, their weaknesses became evident.
                •	Only when Britain later joined the alliance did it become a true counterbalance to the Triple Alliance.

    - The Status of the Triple Alliance
        The Triple Alliance consisted of Germany, Austria-Hungary, and Italy, but internal tensions and conflicting interests weakened its unity.
        6.1. Austria-Hungary: A Fragile Partner
            •	Apart from Germany, the most significant power in the alliance was Austria-Hungary.
            •	As a multiethnic empire, it faced challenges from rising nationalism.
            •	Emperor Franz Josef followed a moderate policy:
                •	He refused to impose military dictatorship in Bohemia.
                •	However, Germany and Hungary formed an unholy alliance to undermine the empire’s balance.
            •	For Germany, Austria-Hungary was merely:
                •	A recruitment ground for its army.
                •	A buffer state against Russian expansion.
            •	Austria-Hungary adopted anti-Slav policies to align with German interests, despite many Slavs being loyal subjects.
            •	This approach also suited Hungary, which had its own record of mistreating minorities.
        6.2. Italy: A Reluctant Ally
            •	Italy joined the Triple Alliance despite its historical enmity with Austria-Hungary.
            •	Its decision was driven by hostility towards France due to:
                •	France’s imperialist policies in Africa.
                •	Mediterranean territorial disputes.
            •	Italy resented French control over:
                •	Savoy
                •	Nice
                •	Corsica
            •	Though a formal member, Italy’s commitment to the alliance remained weak, as its national interests often clashed with those of its allies.

    - The Status of the Ottoman Empire
        The Ottoman Empire sprawled across the Balkans, the Near East, and North Africa. Despite its declining power, it remained a force capable of maintaining its vast, multiethnic dominions.
        7.1. The “Sick Man of Europe”
            •	Tsar Nicholas I famously referred to the Ottoman Empire as the “Sick Man of Europe”, highlighting its weakening position.
            •	However, the empire still managed to hold its diverse territories together, despite internal and external challenges.
        7.2. Military Strength and Resilience
            •	In the late 19th century, the Ottomans proved their resilience by:
            •	Crushing an Armenian revolt.
            •	Defeating Greece decisively in battle.
        •	These victories demonstrated that, although in decline, the Ottoman Empire remained a significant military power in the region.

    - The Moroccan Question
        The Anglo-French Entente focused on resolving Mediterranean disputes, including control over territories such as Morocco, Bosnia, Herzegovina, Tripoli, and the Balkan Peninsula.
        8.1. France’s Blunder and Germany’s Reaction
            •	The Entente granted France a free hand in Morocco, but French Foreign Minister Delcassé failed to consult Germany on the matter.
            •	German Chancellor Bernhard von Bülow took advantage of this to assert that Morocco was an international issue, not just a French concern.
            •	Germany also used the dispute to test the strength of the Anglo-French Entente.
        8.2. Kaiser Wilhelm II’s Intervention (March 1895)
            •	Kaiser Wilhelm II publicly challenged France’s role in Morocco, delivering a speech to the German colony in Tangier:
                •	He emphasized Morocco’s independence and called for an open-door policy for international trade, opposing monopolies or annexation.
            •	In April 1895, Bülow escalated tensions by demanding a conference of the Treaty of Madrid (1880) signatories to discuss Morocco.
        8.3. The Algeciras Conference (1906)
            •	Sultan Abdul Aziz IV of Morocco invited the concerned parties to a conference.
            •	French Foreign Minister Delcassé resisted but eventually conceded under pressure from U.S. President Theodore Roosevelt, marking the first major U.S. intervention in European diplomacy.
            •	The conference was held in Algeciras (January–April 1906).
        8.4. Outcomes of the Conference
            •	France and Spain jointly gained control over the Moroccan police.
            •	Germany ensured that the Moroccan issue remained a multilateral concern.
            •	The conference had broader geopolitical effects:
                •	Strengthened ties between Britain, France, and Russia.
                •	Marked the rise of the U.S. as a world power.
                •	Continued Anglo-French military consultations until 1914, despite changes in the British government.

    - The Anglo-Russian Convention (1907)
        The Anglo-Russian Convention, signed on 31 August 1907, marked the end of longstanding rivalries between Britain and Russia, bringing them to an agreement on key territorial and political issues in Persia, Afghanistan, and Tibet.
        9.1. Persia: Spheres of Influence
            •	Persia was divided into three zones:
                •	A large Russian sphere in the north.
                •	A small British sphere in the southeast.
                •	A neutral zone in between, where both powers were placed on equal footing.
            •	Both countries agreed to maintain the status quo in the Persian Gulf.
        9.2. Afghanistan: British Supremacy Recognized
            •	Britain assured Russia that it would not take any action threatening Russian interests in Afghanistan.
            •	Russia, in turn, agreed to conduct all political relations with Kabul only through the British Foreign Office.
        9.3. Tibet: Non-Interference Policy
            •	Both nations agreed to respect Tibet’s territorial integrity.
            •	They also pledged not to interfere in Tibet’s internal administration.
        The Anglo-Russian Convention marked a significant step in the formation of the Triple Entente, aligning Britain, France, and Russia against the Triple Alliance (Germany, Austria-Hungary, and Italy) in the lead-up to World War I.

    - The Awakening of the Far East
        At the turn of the 19th century, the Far East gained global significance, with the balance of power shifting in favor of Japan. 
            Japan pursued regional supremacy, challenging European interests, particularly in China and Korea.
        10.1. The Boxer Rebellion and Its Aftermath (1900-1901)
            •	The rise of ultra-nationalist Boxers, supported by the Dowager Empress Cixi (1835-1908), threatened foreigners in China.
            •	European powers, setting aside their rivalries, sent an international force to suppress the rebellion in 1900.
            •	The Boxer settlement (September 1901) imposed severe penalties on China:
                •	Emperor Guangxu apologized to Berlin and Tokyo for the murder of German and Japanese diplomats.
                •	China was forced to pay an indemnity of 450,000,000 Taels.
                •	Harsh punishments were imposed:
                    •	Two Imperial princes were sentenced to death.
                    •	Three high-ranking officials were ordered to commit suicide.
                    •	Three leading Mandarins were beheaded.
        10.2. The Anglo-Japanese Alliance (1902)
            •	In January 1902, Britain and Japan signed an agreement to safeguard their special interests in China and Korea.
            •	The treaty allowed Britain to shift its naval focus from the Pacific to the North Sea and Atlantic, where Germany’s growing naval power posed a challenge.
            •	Japan’s international standing soared, reaching its peak in 1905 when it defeated Russia decisively in the Russo-Japanese War.
        This period marked Japan’s emergence as a dominant power in Asia, while European influence in the Far East began to wane.

    - The Russo-Japanese War and the Treaty of Portsmouth (1904-1905)
        By the turn of the 20th century, Russian influence was expanding in northern China. This expansion, particularly in Manchuria and Korea, alarmed Japan and led to rising tensions between the two powers.
        11.1. Rising Tensions Between Russia and Japan
            •	Russian Expansion in Manchuria and Korea
                •	Russia deployed troops into Manchuria and constructed railways linking Vladivostok and Port Arthur under permission from the Chinese government.
                •	In winter 1902-03, Russia increased its activities in North Korea, moving troops towards the Yalu River under the pretext of protecting Russian nationals cutting timber under a Chinese concession.
            •	Failed Diplomatic Negotiations
                •	Negotiations between Russia and Japan led to an agreement in January 1904:
                    •	Japan agreed to stay out of Manchuria.
                    •	Russia agreed to stay out of Korea.
                •	However, Russia failed to respond within the stipulated three weeks, leading Japan to sever diplomatic relations.
        11.2. The Russo-Japanese War (1904-1905)
            •	Contrary to European expectations, Japan was well-prepared:
                •	Its army was expanded, reorganized, and re-equipped.
                •	Its navy was highly efficient and well-organized.
                •	Japan had a geographical advantage, whereas Russia struggled to reinforce its Far Eastern troops.
            •	Economic Backing
                •	Japan received financial support from London and New York.
                •	Russia relied on France.
            •	Major Battles and Japanese Victories
                •	Land Battles: Japan defeated Russian forces in the battles of Liaoyang, Shaho, and Mukden (20 February – 10 March 1905).
                •	Naval Battles:
                    •	Admiral Togo led the destruction of Russian sea power in Port Arthur.
                    •	He inflicted a crushing defeat on Russia’s Baltic Fleet in the Battle of Tsushima (27-28 May 1905).
        11.3. The Treaty of Portsmouth (5 September 1905)
            By summer 1905, Japan had achieved its war objectives, leading to a peace settlement brokered by the United States. 
            The Treaty of Portsmouth (signed 5 September 1905) included:
            •	Russian Recognition of Japanese Dominance
                •	Russia recognized Japan’s paramount interests in Korea.
                •	Russia transferred the Liao-Tung Peninsula lease and the southern Chinese Eastern Railway (Port Arthur to Changchun) to Japan, subject to Chinese approval.
                •	Russia ceded the southern half of Sakhalin Island to Japan.
            •	Withdrawal from Manchuria
                •	Both sides agreed to withdraw their forces from Manchuria and not use railways there for strategic purposes.
        11.4. Consequences of the War
            •	Impact on Russia
                •	The defeat led to domestic rebellion, planting the seeds of the Bolshevik Revolution (1917).
            •	Impact on European Power Balance
                •	Germany’s military strategists saw war on two fronts (as in 1914) as more feasible after Russia’s defeat.
            •	Impact on Nationalist Movements
                •	Japan’s victory over a European power inspired nationalist movements in India, Turkey, Persia, and beyond.
            •	The Anglo-Japanese Alliance (1905)
                •	Britain recognized Japan’s dominance in Korea.
                •	Japan acknowledged Britain’s interests in India’s frontier regions.
        The Russo-Japanese War marked the first time an Asian power decisively defeated a European one, shifting the global balance of power and reinforcing Japan’s role as a dominant force in East Asia.

    The Manchurian Crisis
        •	Japanese Expansion in Korea
            •	Agreement with Korea (17 November 1905)
                •	Japan signed an agreement with Korea.
                •	Prince Ito was appointed as the Resident-General at Seoul.
            •	Assassination of Prince Ito (1909)
                •	Killed by a Korean nationalist.
                •	Rising hostility due to Japanese overbearing and exploitative conduct.
            •	Annexation of Korea (22 August 1910)
                •	Japan formally annexed Korea.
                •	Tacit approval from Russia and Great Britain.
        •	Japanese Expansion in Manchuria
            •	Impact of the Russo-Japanese War
                •	Japan’s victory over Russia strengthened its position in Manchuria.
                •	China was forced to accept the terms of the Portsmouth Treaty.
            •	Sino-Japanese Agreement (22 December 1905)
                •	China agreed to open sixteen towns in Manchuria to foreign trade and residence.
                •	Japan was allowed to reconstruct railways connecting Antung (Korean frontier) with Mukden.
            •	Friction and Delays
                •	Reconstruction was completed only in 1911 due to tensions.
                •	China tried to involve Britain and the US by granting railway concessions to British-American companies.
        •	US Intervention and Its Failure
            •	US Proposal (1909)
                •	Suggested an international loan to China to buy foreign-controlled Manchurian railways.
                •	Aimed at ensuring railway neutralization.
            •	Rejection by Russia and Japan
                •	Russia and Japan jointly rejected the US proposal.
                •	Showed that the US was still far from becoming a world power.
        •	Growing Japan-US Tensions
            •	Speculation of Conflict
                •	Rising tensions over immigration issues between Japan and the US.
            •	Third Anglo-Japanese Alliance (1911)
                •	Strengthened Japan’s position in the Far East.
        •	China’s Political Collapse
        •	Government Breakdown
            •	Administrative failure across all provinces.
        •	Fall of the Manchu Dynasty (1912)
            •	Yuan Shih-Kai took power.
            •	Established a new regime, ending imperial rule in China.

    Crisis in the Near East
        1. The Aftermath of the Treaty of Berlin (1878)
            •	The Treaty of Berlin settled Near Eastern affairs, with little change except for Egypt coming under British rule.
            •	The Ottoman Empire, though in decline, failed to capitalize on the internal divisions within the Balkans.
            •	Britain’s acquisition of Cyprus and dominance over Egypt reduced Ottoman Turkey’s strategic importance.
            •	Russia, after the Treaty of Portsmouth (1905), shifted focus from the Near East to the Middle East, creating a power vacuum that Germany sought to exploit.
        2. The Armenian Massacres and German Influence
            •	During the late 19th century, Kaiser Wilhelm II’s interest in the region became evident, particularly during the Armenian massacres.
            •	The massacres at Urfa (September 1895) and Constantinople (August 1896) were orchestrated under Sultan Abdul Hamid II, with Turkish soldiers and fanatics carrying out the atrocities.
            •	The British public reacted strongly, but Salisbury’s Conservative government refrained from intervention to avoid a European war.
            •	The crisis strengthened German influence in Constantinople while weakening Britain’s position, setting the stage for World War I.
        3. The Macedonian Crisis (1903-1908)
            •	Turkish misrule in Macedonia led to tensions, prompting Austria and Russia to agree on the Murzsteg reforms in 1903, dividing Macedonia into administrative zones.
            •	Austria’s 1908 proposal to build a railway through the Sanjak of Novibazar raised Russian suspicions, ending their cooperation in the Balkans.
            •	In July 1908, the Young Turk Revolution, led by Enver Pasha, overthrew Sultan Abdul Hamid II and established a parliamentary system.
            •	The new Parliament’s inclusion of representatives from Bosnia and Herzegovina alarmed Vienna, as Austria-Hungary formally occupied these provinces.
        4. The Annexation of Bosnia and Herzegovina (1908)
            •	Serbia, under King Peter I, sought the unification of Southern Slavs, but Austria-Hungary swiftly moved to annex Bosnia and Herzegovina.
            •	A secret agreement between Austria and Russia allowed the annexation in exchange for Russian access to the Bosphorus and the Dardanelles.
            •	On 5 October 1908, Bulgaria declared independence, and the next day, Austria-Hungary annexed Bosnia and Herzegovina.
            •	Serbia, diplomatically isolated, had to accept the annexation in March 1909.
            •	German Chancellor Bülow hailed this as proof of the Austro-German alliance’s strength in continental politics.
        5. The Moroccan Crisis (1908-1912)
            •	In 1908, Sultan Abdul Aziz IV of Morocco was overthrown by his brother Mulai Hafid, but his rule was unstable.
            •	The Riff tribes rebelled against Spain in 1909, forcing Spain to deploy 50,000 troops.
            •	In 1911, revolts around Fez led France to intervene, occupying the city while Spain seized Larache.
            •	Germany, angered by these developments, sent the warship Panther to Agadir, challenging the Treaty of Algeciras.
            •	Britain reacted sharply, leading to negotiations where Germany backed down.
            •	In 1912, France established a protectorate over Morocco, ceding parts of the French Congo to Germany in return.
            •	The crisis left lasting tensions between Britain, France, and Germany.
        6. The Italo-Turkish War (1911-1912)
            •	Italy, fearing French expansion in North Africa, declared war on Turkey in September 1911.
            •	Using Turkish interference in trade as a pretext, Italy seized Tripoli.
            •	Britain denied Turkey permission to send reinforcements through Egypt, allowing Italy an easy victory.
            •	The Treaty of Ouchy (October 1912) resulted in Turkey ceding Tripoli to Italy.
            •	Italy’s shift from the Triple Alliance to the Triple Entente was a crucial development for World War I.
        7. Formation of the Balkan League (1912)
            •	Bulgaria’s King Ferdinand and Prime Minister Gueshoff sought cooperation with Serbia to realize Bulgaria’s territorial ambitions.
            •	With Russian support, Serbia and Bulgaria signed a treaty in March 1912, joined by Greece in May and Montenegro in August.
            •	A secret annex in the treaty provided for joint military action against Turkey under Russian approval.
        8. The First Balkan War (1912-1913)
            •	Turkish promises of reform in Albania dissatisfied Serbia and Bulgaria.
            •	Montenegro mobilized, and by 3 November 1912, Balkan Christian states were at war with Turkey.
            •	The Bulgarians advanced toward Constantinople, forcing Turkey to seek European intervention.
            •	A conference in London led to a peace treaty in December 1912, but Enver Pasha rejected it.
            •	Fighting resumed, with Adrianople falling to Bulgarian and Serbian forces while Greece captured Jannina.
            •	The Treaty of May 1913:
                •	Greece gained Salonika, southern Macedonia, and Crete.
                •	Serbia secured northern Macedonia.
                •	Bulgaria received Thrace and the Aegean coast but ceded Silistria to Romania.
                •	Turkey retained Constantinople and eastern Thrace.
        9. The Second Balkan War (1913)
            •	Within a month, Bulgaria attacked Serbian forces in Macedonia and attempted to seize Salonika.
            •	Serbia and Greece resisted, while Romania invaded Bulgaria.
            •	Turkey exploited the situation to retake Adrianople.
            •	The Treaty of Bucharest (August 1913):
                •	Bulgaria lost access to the Aegean Sea and territory in Macedonia.
                •	Romania gained land with 340,000 people.
                •	Turkey retained Adrianople.
                •	Albania was recognized as independent.
        10. Prelude to World War I (1913-1914)
            •	The Balkan Wars proved that Britain and Germany could cooperate to prevent war.
            •	However, military buildup intensified across Europe, with growing tensions in Germany and France.
            •	French Ambassador to Vienna noted the rising belief that war was inevitable.
        11. The Assassination of Archduke Franz Ferdinand (28 June 1914)
            •	The heir to the Habsburg throne was assassinated in Sarajevo, triggering a chain of events leading to World War I.
            •	Austria issued an ultimatum to Serbia on 23 July 1914, demanding compliance within 48 hours.
            •	On 31 July, Germany gave Russia an ultimatum to halt mobilization within 12 hours.
            •	By 1 August, Germany and Russia were at war, followed by Germany’s declaration of war on France on 3 August.
            •	Germany’s invasion of Belgium provoked Britain, which declared war on 4-5 August.
            •	Bismarck’s 1891 prediction that a world war would start in the East proved correct.

    - World War I
        Causes of World War I
            The immediate provocation for World War I was the assassination of Archduke Francis Ferdinand. 
            However, several underlying causes had been brewing for over a generation, leading to the outbreak of the war:
                Extreme Nationalism
                    Inflamed racial pride among people of different nations.
                    Increased rivalries between states.
                    Led to disputes over colonial possessions in Asia, Africa, and the Balkans.
                    Nationalist aspirations in the Balkans turned the region into a volatile "tinderbox" that ultimately ignited the war.
                Formation of Alliances
                    Nations grouped into mutually suspicious alliances, increasing tensions.
                    Militarism, along with secret treaties, intensified rivalries, setting the stage for war.
                Imperialism and Economic Conflicts
                    Competition for markets, raw materials, and investment opportunities.
                    Germany’s unfulfilled imperialist ambitions played a major role in the war’s outbreak in 1914.
        Character and Progress of World War I
            When war broke out, it became unprecedented in scale and destruction:
                Nearly all known nation-states participated, and battles were fought worldwide.
                Lethal weaponry, including submarines, Zeppelins, poison gas, tanks, and armored vehicles, led to massive casualties.
                Like many European wars before, it was fought for the "Balance of Power."
                Two opposing sides emerged:
                    Central Powers: Germany, Austria-Hungary, Bulgaria, and Turkey.
                    Allied Powers: France, England, Japan, and Italy.
        Key Developments:
            For the first three and a half years, Germany held the upper hand.
            In 1917, Russia withdrew from the war after the Bolsheviks overthrew the Tsarist regime, further strengthening Germany.
            Germany's large-scale submarine warfare (U-boat menace) threatened Allied control of the seas.
            The United States entered the war after a German submarine sank the American ship Lusitania, turning the tide in favor of the Allies.
            Germany surrendered in November 1918 due to combined Allied pressure.
            The Allies quickly defeated Turkey, secured Bulgaria's surrender, and witnessed Austria's collapse.
        End of the War:
            President Woodrow Wilson of the USA proposed the "Fourteen Points" as the basis for a peace settlement.
            A naval mutiny in Germany led to the Kaiser fleeing to Holland.
            A republic was established in Germany, which signed the armistice on 11 November 1918, ending the war.
        The Paris Peace Conference of 1919
            Held in Paris in 1919, attended by the victorious Allied powers.
            The venue selection reflected a spirit of vengeance.
            The conference had to consider various secret treaties made during the war regarding territorial distributions.
            French Prime Minister Clemenceau and British Premier Lloyd George pressured Wilson into compromising on his Fourteen Points.
            Resulted in a series of five treaties concluded in and around Paris.
        The Treaty of Versailles, 1919
            Signed between the Allies and Germany on 28 June 1919, the Treaty of Versailles imposed harsh conditions on Germany:
                Territorial Losses:
                    Alsace-Lorraine was returned to France.
                    Saar Valley’s coal fields were given to France for 15 years.
                    Eupen and Malmedy were ceded to Belgium after a plebiscite.
                    Schleswig was returned to Denmark following a plebiscite.
                    West Prussia was used to create a Polish corridor to the Baltic.
                    Memel was given to Lithuania, and Prussian Poland was annexed to Poland.
                    Danzig was internationalized under League of Nations control.
                Colonial and Overseas Losses:
                    Germany lost all African colonies and privileges in China, which were placed under the League of Nations.
                Military Restrictions:
                    German army reduced to 100,000 men; conscription abolished.
                    Banned the production and trade of war materials.
                    German navy was limited and prohibited from having submarines.
                    The Rhineland was demilitarized.
                War Guilt and Reparations:
                    Germany accepted full responsibility for the war.
                    Had to pay huge financial reparations.
                    Allied forces occupied the left bank of the Rhine until treaty terms were met.
                The Treaty of Versailles was a "dictated peace" as German representatives had no say in its terms.
        Other Treaties
            Treaty of St. Germain (1919): Signed with Austria-Hungary, dismembering the empire:
                Bohemia and Moravia formed Czechoslovakia.
                Bosnia and Herzegovina merged with Serbia to form Yugoslavia.
                Italy received South Tyrol, Trentino, and parts of the Adriatic coast.
                Poland received Galicia, and Hungary became independent.
            Treaty of Neuilly (1919): Bulgaria lost:
                Western Thrace to Greece.
                Part of Macedonia to Yugoslavia.
                Dobruja to Romania.
            Treaty of Trianon (1920): Hungary ceded:
                Transylvania to Romania.
                Slovakia to Czechoslovakia.
                Croatia to Yugoslavia, losing all sea access.
            Treaty of Sèvres (1920): Signed with Turkey:
                Lost all rights in Africa and the Middle East.
                Greece occupied Eastern Thrace, Smyrna, and parts of Asia Minor.
                Armenia was to become independent.
                Dardanelles and Bosphorus became neutral zones.
                Turkish nationalist leader Mustafa Kemal rejected the treaty, leading to its replacement by the Treaty of Lausanne.
        Results of the War
            Collapse of four major empires: German, Austro-Hungarian, Ottoman, and Russian.
            Rise of new republics and smaller nation-states.
            Spread of democratic ideals, but also rise of totalitarian ideologies (Bolshevism, Fascism, and Nazism).
            Formation of the League of Nations to resolve future conflicts.
            Advances in labor rights, women's suffrage, medicine, aviation, and military technology.
        Impact of World War I on India
            India actively supported Britain:
                300,000 Indian soldiers fought in West Asia, Africa, and Europe.
                800,000 more were later recruited.
                Supplied vast amounts of food, materials, and funds.
                Contributed £100 million to Britain’s war costs.
            Britain’s Response:
                Promised self-governance (Montagu Declaration, 20 August 1917).
                Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms (Government of India Act, 1919) introduced dyarchy.
                Failed to satisfy Indian aspirations, especially after oppressive measures:
                    Rowlatt Act: Allowed arrests without trial.
                    Jallianwala Bagh Massacre (1919): British troops killed peaceful protestors.
                    Khilafat Movement: Discontent over British treatment of Turkey.
            Rise of Indian nationalism led by Mahatma Gandhi, ultimately culminating in India's independence on 15 August 1947.

    The Russian Revolution, 1917
        1. Introduction
            During World War I, two significant events occurred in 1917:
                •	The entry of the United States into the war, which ultimately influenced its outcome.
                •	The Russian Revolution, which marked the end of the Tsarist regime and introduced the “dictatorship of the proletariat”.
            The Russian Revolution was the most significant social upheaval since the French Revolution.
        2. Circumstances Leading to the Russian Revolution
            The revolution, epitomized by the Bolshevik seizure of power in Petrograd on 7 November 1917, had long been in the making due to:
                •	The Russo-Japanese War (1905), which weakened confidence in the Tsarist regime.
                •	Russian military defeats in World War I, leading to further distrust.
                •	Widespread discontent with the inefficient and autocratic rule of the Tsar.
        3. The First Revolution (March 1917)
            Initially, Russia entered the war with enthusiasm and saw some early successes. However, by 1915:
                •	Military defeats exposed the regime’s weaknesses.
                •	Munitions and supplies were critically low.
                •	Tsar Nicholas II was weak and influenced by corrupt ministers.
                •	Rumors of a separate peace with Germany fueled unrest.
                •	Rasputin’s influence over the royal family worsened the crisis, leading to his assassination.
            Key Events:
                •	Riots erupted among peasants and workers.
                •	Desertions increased within the army.
                •	Food shortages intensified, particularly a shortage of bread.
                •	The Petrograd Soviet (workers’ and soldiers’ council) was formed.
                •	The Duma (parliament) established a Provisional Government, forcing Tsar Nicholas II to abdicate.
        4. The Provisional Government
            The Provisional Government, led by moderate republicans (Kadets), implemented:
                •	Liberal reforms (freedom of press, speech, and religion).
                •	Plans for a National Constituent Assembly.
                •	Commitment to continuing the war, hoping to maintain patriotic support.
            However, the masses prioritized:
                •	Peace
                •	Land reforms
                •	Food security
            Growing Discontent:
                •	The revolution shifted from a political movement to a socialist one.
                •	Local Soviets emerged across Russia, mobilizing workers, peasants, and soldiers.
                •	Workers demanded better wages and working conditions.
                •	Peasants seized noble lands.
                •	Soldiers mutinied, and national minorities (Finns, Poles) sought independence.
        5. The Second Revolution (November 1917)
            The Rise of the Bolsheviks:
                •	The failure of the Provisional Government to end the war led to dissatisfaction.
                •	Mensheviks, under Alexander Kerensky, came to power but failed to bring peace.
                •	The Bolsheviks, led by Lenin and Trotsky, opposed the war and advocated immediate socialist reforms.
            The Bolshevik Coup:
                •	Germans advanced towards Petrograd, creating instability.
                •	Bolshevik influence grew within the Petrograd Soviet.
                •	In November 1917, the Bolsheviks overthrew the Provisional Government.
                •	Kerensky fled, and Bolsheviks seized power.
        6. Consolidation of Bolshevik Rule\
            Immediate Actions:
                •	Lenin negotiated the Treaty of Brest-Litovsk (1918), ending Russia’s involvement in World War I.
                •	Territorial losses were accepted as a necessary price for revolution.
            Key Reforms:
                •	Abolition of private property.
                •	Peasants received land to be cultivated for the state.
                •	Factories were handed over to workers.
                •	Compulsory labor was introduced.
                •	Public debts were repudiated.
                •	The Orthodox Church was stripped of power.
            Repression:
                •	The Cheka (secret police) executed opposition figures, including Tsar Nicholas II and his family (July 1918).
                •	The Red Army, led by Trotsky, crushed anti-Bolshevik forces, ensuring Bolshevik rule for over 70 years.
        7. Causes of the Russian Revolution
            The revolution resulted from long-term dissatisfaction across Russian society:
            1. Peasants
                •	Suffered economic insecurity and social oppression.
                •	Small land holdings vs. large aristocratic estates led to resentment.
                •	Formed the bulk of the Social Revolutionary Party.
            2. Workers
                •	Low wages and harsh working conditions led to discontent.
                •	Many joined the Workmen’s Social Democratic Party.
            3. Middle Class (Bourgeoisie)
                •	Wanted constitutional government to limit autocratic rule.
            4. Intellectuals
                •	Advocated radical political and social reform.
            5. Minority Nationalities (Jews, Poles, Finns, etc.)
                •	Resisted Tsarist policies of Russification and sought independence.
            6. Influence of Ideologies
                •	Moderate republicanism and Marxian socialism fueled revolutionary movements.
            These diverse elements ultimately overthrew the Tsarist regime, leading to the establishment of a socialist state under the Bolsheviks.


- The world from 1919 to 1939
    The Inter-War Period (1919–1939): Prelude to World War II
        - Aftermath of World War I
            •	World War I, or the Great War, was called the ‘war to end all wars.’
            •	It was the most devastating conflict in human history up to that time.
            •	Over 37 million combatants lost their lives.
            •	None of the original parties to the war achieved anything substantial.
            •	Despite the devastation, the war ended with a sense of hope.
        - Post-War Optimism
            •	Many expected that humanity would learn from the horrors of the war.
            •	It was hoped that future territorial disputes would be resolved through structured dispute-settlement mechanisms.
            •	There was optimism that democracy would be safeguarded.
            •	Hopes of equity and progress for all were widespread.
        - Reality: Continued Chaos and Disillusionment
            •	Contrary to expectations, Europe quickly fell into further chaos.
            •	The United States and Europe remained the political and economic centers of global affairs.
            •	Instead of peace and stability, disillusionment spread across the world.
        - Persistence of Pre-War Institutions
            •	Colonialism remained intact, keeping over half of the world’s population in subjugation.
            •	Aristocracy, feudalism, supra-nationalism, and militarism continued to exist.
            •	Rather than fading away, these forces shaped the political landscape of the post-war world.
        - Rise of New Political Forces
            •	Fascism and Communism emerged as dominant ideologies.
            •	These forces played a crucial role in destabilizing Europe.
            •	The continent, and consequently the world, moved closer to another catastrophic war.
        - The ‘Inter-War’ Period (1919–1939)
            •	The period between World War I and World War II is known as the ‘inter-war’ period.
            •	During this time, major world powers and their allies influenced global politics.
            •	Their actions collectively contributed to the conditions that led to World War II.
            •	This chapter analyzes their roles in the unfolding global crisis.

    The League of Nations: Mankind’s First Global Peacekeeping Attempt
        1. Introduction
            •	Any discussion on the inter-war period must begin with the League of Nations.
            •	It was humanity’s first attempt at creating a global institution to prevent armed conflict.
            •	The League aimed to serve as a neutral arbiter in international disputes while preserving national sovereignty.
        2. Origin and Vision
            •	The League of Nations was originally the vision of U.S. President Woodrow Wilson (1913–21).
            •	He proposed its formation in his Fourteen Points on 18 January 1918.
            •	The victorious powers incorporated the Covenant of the League of Nations into the peace treaties that concluded World War I.
            •	It was designed as a forum where all states, regardless of size, could cooperate to maintain peace and limit military aggression.
        3. Formation and Structure
            •	The Covenant of the League of Nations outlined its principles and operational framework.
            •	An international committee, including leaders like Lord Robert Cecil (Britain), Jan Smuts (South Africa), and Léon Bourgeois (France), drafted the Covenant.
            •	President Wilson ensured that the Covenant was included in every peace treaty, securing its establishment.
            •	Headquarters: Geneva, Switzerland.
            •	Main Organs:
                •	General Assembly – Included all member states.
                •	Council – Had four permanent members: Britain, France, Italy, and Japan.
                •	Permanent Court of International Justice – Based in The Hague, Netherlands.
                •	Secretariat – Managed administrative affairs.
                •	Various Committees – Focused on mandates, dispute resolution, and disarmament.
                •	Peacekeeping Arm – Responsible for conflict resolution.
                •	International Labour Organisation (ILO) – Created under the League’s mandate.
                •	Refugee and Health Organisations – Precursors to modern United Nations (UN) agencies.
        4. Fundamental Weaknesses and Contradictions
            •	The League was flawed from the beginning.
            •	It did not include a clear stance against colonialism.
            •	Instead, it introduced a “mandate” system, where colonies were placed under the control of major powers under the pretext of guiding them toward independence.
            •	Lack of enforcement mechanisms made this provision ineffective.
            •	To persuade Japan to join, it allowed Japan to take over former German concessions in China.
            •	To appease France, Germany and Russia were excluded from membership.
            •	The United States refused to join, further weakening the League.
            •	As a result, three of the world’s most powerful nations (USA, Germany, and Russia) had no role in the League, significantly diminishing its impact.
        5. Failures of the League
            •	The League repeatedly failed to act in major international crises:
            1.	1920 - Polish Seizure of Vilna:
                France-backed Poland seized the Vilna region from Lithuania, and the League did not intervene.
            2.	1923 - Corfu Incident:
                •	Three Italian officials were murdered on Greek territory.
                •	Italian dictator Benito Mussolini blamed Greece and demanded compensation.
                •	Greece refused, leading to an Italian bombing campaign on Corfu.
                •	Instead of League intervention, Britain and France acted as mediators.
            3.	1931 – Japanese Invasion of Manchuria: 
                Japan seized Manchuria from China in violation of international agreements.
            4.	1936 – Italian Invasion of Ethiopia: 
                Italy conquered Ethiopia, and the League did nothing.
            5.	1930s – Rise of Adolf Hitler: 
                By the time Hitler emerged as a European threat, secret treaties and power-balancing tactics had returned, rendering the League obsolete.
        6. Legacy of the League of Nations
            •	The League provided a model for the United Nations (UN), which was established after World War II.
            •	However, during its existence, it failed to prevent major conflicts and proved ineffective in maintaining peace.
            •	With the League’s decline, global politics reverted to military alliances, secret treaties, and power struggles, setting the stage for World War II.
            

    Germany
        1. The Impact of the Treaty of Versailles on Germany
            1.1. The Treaty’s Effect on German Pride
                •	The Treaty of Versailles left a deep scar on German pride.
                •	Many Germans believed they had been “stabbed in the back” by their own generals and communists at the end of World War I.
                •	This resentment fueled the desire for revenge, contributing to the conditions leading to World War II.
            1.2. A Historical Perspective on Post-War Punishments
                •	Retaliatory actions after conflicts were not uncommon in European history.
                •	The Congress of Vienna (1815) had imposed heavy penalties on France for the Napoleonic Wars.
                •	Germany had treated France harshly in 1871 after the Franco-Prussian War.
                •	The Treaty of Brest-Litovsk (1918) had imposed severe territorial and economic losses on Russia by Germany.
                •	Compared to Brest-Litovsk, Versailles was lenient—Germany remained geographically and economically intact and preserved its political unity.
        2. The Weimar Republic: Struggles and Challenges
            2.1. Formation of the Weimar Republic
                •	Germany’s Chancellor Philip Scheidemann called the Treaty an “imprisonment of the German people” but had no choice but to accept it.
                •	The Social Democrats and the Catholic Centre Party formed a new government, signing the Treaty under pressure from the victorious Allies.
                •	The new government, known as the Weimar Republic (1919–1933), was stigmatized for accepting Versailles under threat of military occupation.
            2.2. The Weimar Constitution
                •	A liberal document promising civil liberties and universal adult suffrage.
                •	However, it had structural flaws:
                    •	Proportional representation made it easy for small parties to gain seats, destabilizing governance.
                    •	The President had the power to appoint or remove the Chancellor, who led the cabinet.
                    •	The President could rule by decree in national emergencies, enabling dictatorship.
        3. Political Instability and Extremist Threats
            3.1. Left-Wing and Right-Wing Uprisings
                •	The Republic faced constant threats from both left-wing communists and right-wing conservatives.
                •	In 1919, the Spartacist uprising, led by Rosa Luxemburg and Karl Liebknecht, attempted a communist revolution but was crushed by the Army. Both leaders were executed.
                •	In 1920, the right-wing Kapp Putsch tried to restore Prussian aristocratic dominance, causing government officials to flee Berlin temporarily.
                •	Subsequent strikes and uprisings required military intervention.
                •	Assassinations of key Republican leaders further destabilized Germany.
        4. Economic Crises and Their Political Impact
            4.1. The Burden of War Reparations
                •	In 1921, the Allies presented Germany with a war reparations bill of over $33 billion, forcing the Weimar government to accept under threat of invasion.
                •	The French invaded the Ruhr in 1923 to enforce payments, prompting passive resistance—German workers went on strike while the government subsidized them.
            4.2. Hyperinflation and Unemployment
                •	Printing excessive currency led to hyperinflation.
                •	Unemployment reached 6 million in the mid-1920s.
                •	Salaries could not keep up with rising prices.
                •	Those with fixed incomes suffered the most.
            4.3. The Great Depression’s Impact (1929)
                •	Germany’s economic revival had relied on American loans and cheap exports under the Dawes Plan (1924) and the Young Plan (1929).
                •	The Wall Street Crash (1929) caused an economic collapse, further weakening the Republic.
                •	Agricultural prices collapsed, angering farmers.
                •	University students faced disillusionment due to unemployment.
        5. Weimar Republic’s Brief Stability and Stresemann’s Efforts
            5.1. Gustav Stresemann’s Leadership (1923)
                •	Briefly served as Chancellor before becoming Foreign Minister.
                •	Stabilized the economy by appointing economist Hjalmar Schacht, who introduced a new currency (Rentenmark).
                •	Negotiated new reparation payment methods, leading to the withdrawal of French forces from the Ruhr.
            5.2. Foreign Policy Achievements
                •	Pursued reconciliation with Britain and France.
                •	Germany joined the League of Nations.
                •	Negotiated the Treaty of Locarno (1925), which secured Germany’s western borders but left eastern borders ambiguous—favoring future territorial revision.
                •	However, nationalist parties, including Hitler’s Nazi Party, opposed Locarno, seeing it as an extension of Versailles.
            5.3. The End of Weimar and the Rise of Totalitarianism
                •	Despite Stresemann’s efforts, the Great Depression provided an opportunity for radical political forces.
                •	Economic desperation led to a shift towards totalitarian ideologies.
                •	Hitler and the Nazi Party exploited public discontent to rise to power in 1933, marking the end of the Weimar Republic.
        The Rise of Nazism
            1. Adolf Hitler’s Early Life and Entry into Politics
                •	Adolf Hitler first appeared on the German political scene in 1923. His rise to power was unprecedented.
                •	William L. Shirer, the American journalist-historian, described him as an unlikely figure to succeed the legacy of Bismarck, the Hohenzollern emperors, and President Hindenburg.
                •	Born in 1889 as the son of an Austrian customs official, Hitler had an incomplete education and lived as a vagabond in Vienna.
                •	During World War I, he fought in the German Army with exceptional valor, earning high military honors.
                •	After the war, he lived a purposeless life but was deeply affected by Germany’s humiliation, fueling his desire for revenge and driving him into politics.
            2. Formation of the Nazi Party
                •	In 1919, as an intelligence agent for the German Army, Hitler attended a meeting of a small nationalist and anti-Semitic group and later joined it out of curiosity.
                •	By 1920, the group was renamed the National Socialist German Workers Party (Nazi Party).
                •	The party’s Twenty-Five Points demanded:
                    •	Repudiation of the Treaty of Versailles.
                    •	Unification of Austria and Germany.
                    •	Exclusion of Jews from German citizenship.
                    •	Agrarian reforms and nationalization of industries.
                •	The Nazis conducted their activities with military fanfare, organizing paramilitary groups known as Sturm Abteilung (SA) or Brownshirts.
            3. The 1923 Beer Hall Putsch and Its Aftermath
                •	The French occupation of the Ruhr Valley in 1923 helped the Nazis stir nationalist passions.
                •	On November 9, 1923, Hitler led a failed coup in Munich during a Bavarian legislature meeting at a Beer Hall.
                •	The coup was crushed, and Hitler was sentenced to five years in prison, but he served less than a year before being paroled.
                •	While in jail, he wrote Mein Kampf (My Struggle) and realized that legal means were necessary to capture power.
            4. The Nazis and the Economic Crisis of 1929
                •	After Hitler’s release, extreme nationalism lost appeal under the Weimar Republic’s reformist phase.
                •	The Great Depression of 1929 devastated Germany’s economy, increasing unemployment and political instability.
                •	The centrist coalition government collapsed in March 1930 over economic disagreements.
                •	President Paul von Hindenburg ruled by decree and appointed Heinrich Brüning as Chancellor, but the crisis persisted.
            5. Nazi Electoral Success and Hitler’s Rise to Power
                •	In 1932, von Hindenburg replaced Brüning with Franz von Papen, who sought Nazi support.
                •	The Nazi Party gained 30.1% of votes in 1930, rising from 12 Reichstag seats in 1928 to 107 seats.
                •	To counter opposition, von Papen lifted the ban on Nazi rallies, leading to further electoral gains.
                •	In the July 1932 elections, the Nazis won 230 seats (37.2% of the vote).
                •	Hitler demanded the Chancellorship, but Hindenburg refused.
                •	After political deadlock and von Papen’s resignation, Kurt von Schleicher was appointed Chancellor but dismissed soon after.
                •	On January 30, 1933, Hitler was appointed Chancellor of Germany.
            6. Hitler’s Consolidation of Power
                6.1 Eliminating Political Opposition
                    •	Within a month of taking office, Hitler used the Reichstag Fire incident to suppress communists.
                    •	The fire was later revealed to be set by Nazi agents, but they blamed the communists, stirring fears of a Soviet-style revolution.
                    •	Hitler secured emergency powers and jailed or executed communist leaders.
                    •	In March 1933, the Enabling Act was passed, allowing Hitler to rule by decree.
                6.2 Establishing a One-Party Dictatorship
                    •	Hitler outlawed all political parties by July 1933, making the Nazi Party the only legal party.
                    •	Provincial governments were reorganized to suit Nazi interests.
                    •	Opposition politicians were imprisoned, sent to concentration camps, or murdered.
                    •	Nazi spies monitored civilians, turning Germany into a police state.
                    •	Germans were forced to swear personal loyalty to Hitler, addressed as Der Führer (The Leader).
                6.3 The Suppression of Jews
                    •	May 1933: Jewish intellectuals’ books were burned in public bonfires.
                    •	1935 Nuremberg Laws: German Jews lost their citizenship and were expelled from jobs.
                    •	November 1938 Kristallnacht (Night of Broken Glass):
                        •	Jewish homes, shops, and synagogues were attacked.
                        •	Thousands of Jews were arrested.
                6.4 The Night of the Long Knives (June 30, 1934)
                    •	The SA (Brownshirts), led by Ernst Röhm, had grown to one million members.
                    •	The German Army feared it would become a rival military force.
                    •	To secure the Army’s loyalty, Hitler ordered Röhm’s execution along with key SA leaders.
                    •	Former Chancellor von Schleicher was also killed.
                    •	A month later, President von Hindenburg died, and Hitler merged the offices of President and Chancellor, becoming the supreme dictator.
            7. Hitler’s Militarization and Foreign Policy
                •	Hitler rearmed Germany, violating the Treaty of Versailles.
                •	In 1936, he remilitarized the Rhineland, but France failed to intervene.
                •	He promoted Lebensraum (“living space”), justifying territorial expansion in Eastern Europe.
            8. Conclusion: Germany’s Path to War
                •	Within two decades of World War I, Germany emerged as a threat to European peace.
                •	The Allied powers, preoccupied with domestic problems, did not resist Hitler’s militarization.
                •	Hitler instilled racist ideologies, portraying Germans as Aryans, superior to Jews and Slavs.
                •	This ideology set Germany on the path to another global war.
                •	Meanwhile, a similar authoritarian movement was rising in Italy under Fascism, which we shall now examine.

    1. Italy and the Rise of Fascism
        1.1 Origins and Ideology of Fascism
            Fascism was a political ideology antithetical to democracy
            •	Fascism opposed:
                •	Democracy
                •	Parliamentarism
                •	Individual liberty
                •	Freedom of expression
                •	State control over businesses (though it clashed with communism)
            •	Derived from the Italian word fasces, meaning the authority of the state over all.
            •	Fascists believed:
                •	Democratic politics weakened nations by prioritizing partisan interests over national unity.
                •	Single-party rule was necessary to maintain order and strength.
            •	Benito Mussolini:
                •	First fascist dictator, came to power in 1922.
                •	Declared in 1931:
                        “The Fascist conception of the state is all-embracing, and outside of the state, no human or spiritual values can exist.”
            •	Fascist governments in Italy, Germany, and Spain were characterized by:
                •	Single-party rule
                •	Use of terror
                •	Regimentation of society
                •	Widespread police surveillance
        1.2 Mussolini’s Early Life and Political Rise
            •	Born in 1883, son of a blacksmith.
            •	Entered politics in 1912 as a socialist.
            •	In 1914:
                •	Left socialism.
                •	Joined the Italian Army (Italy fought on the Allied side in WWI).
                •	Was wounded in action.
            •	After WWI (1919):
                •	Founded Fasci di Combattimento (Band of Combatants) in Milan.
            •	Nationalist sentiment grew in Italy due to:
                •	A feeling of betrayal, as the country was denied territorial gains despite being on the victorious side.
                •	Dysfunctional parliamentary system.
                •	Widespread internal turmoil, strikes, and unrest.
                •	Fear of a communist takeover.
        1.3 The Rise of the Black Shirts and Seizure of Power
            •	Mussolini organized the Black Shirts to:
                •	Terrorize Socialists (the most powerful party at the time).
                •	Attack strikers and farm workers.
            •	Gained support from:
                •	Conservatives
                •	Industrialists
            •	1921:
                •	Mussolini and 34 of his followers elected to the Chamber of Deputies.
            •	October 1922:
                •	Organized the March on Rome (hundreds of thousands of Black Shirts participated).
                •	King Victor Emmanuel III, alarmed, sent a telegram on 29 October asking Mussolini to become Prime Minister.
        1.4 Mussolini’s Establishment of Dictatorship
            •	Initially, Mussolini’s Fascists were a minority in the Chamber of Deputies.
            •	He demanded dictatorial powers from the King:
                •	The King, pressured by big business, agreed on 23 November 1922.
                •	Given dictatorial powers for one year to ensure good governance.
            •	Mussolini’s actions to consolidate power:
                •	Removed elected representatives from lower government levels.
                •	Replaced them with Fascists.
            •	1924:
                •	Changed election laws:
                    •	The party with the largest popular vote would automatically receive two-thirds of Parliament seats.
                    •	Coalition governments became obsolete.
            •	1925 elections:
                •	Fascists gained complete control over the Chamber of Deputies.
            •	1925–1926:
                •	Passed laws granting Mussolini:
                    •	Power to rule by decree.
                    •	Authority to dissolve all other political parties.
        1.5 Mussolini’s Policies and Downfall
            •	Ruthless against opponents.
            •	Gained popularity among the masses through:
                •	Large public projects.
                •	Social security for workers.
                •	Subsidized vacations.
            •	1929:
                •	Won over the Catholic Church:
                    •	Gave the Pope sovereignty over the Vatican.
                    •	Made Roman Catholicism the state religion.
            •	Late 1920s:
                •	Strengthened Italy’s economy.
            •	1930s:
                •	Great Depression weakened Italy’s economy.
                •	To maintain power, Mussolini initiated military adventures abroad.
                •	Italy’s aggressive policies contributed to Europe’s instability, leading to World War II.

    1. Spain and the Spanish Civil War
        1.1 Spain Before the Civil War
            1.1.1 Constitutional Monarchy (1885–1923)
                •	Spain was a constitutional monarchy under King Alfonso XIII (ruled since 1885).
            1.1.2 Rule of General Primo de Rivera (1923–1930)
                •	1923: General Primo de Rivera staged a coup and ruled with the King’s approval.
                •	Though a dictator, he was:
                    •	Not a fascist.
                    •	Progressive in implementing public works and industrialization.
                    •	Successfully resolved Spain’s problems in Morocco (1925).
                •	Spain remained stable until the Great Depression affected its economy.
                •	The economic crisis led to:
                    •	Financial ruin.
                    •	Rising unemployment.
                    •	Loss of Army support for de Rivera.
                •	1930: De Rivera resigned, and King Alfonso XIII abdicated.
                •	A Republic was proclaimed.
        1.2 Problems Faced by the New Republic
            •	Political and Regional Conflicts:
                •	Catalonia and the Basque provinces demanded separation.
                •	The Roman Catholic Church opposed the Republic and wanted the King’s return.
            •	Economic Crisis:
                •	Wine and olive export markets collapsed.
                •	Iron and steel production declined.
                •	Unemployment rose.
                •	Public discontent against the new government.
            •	Right-Wing Reforms and Resistance:
                •	Conservative Right-Wing parties controlled the Cortes (Parliament).
                •	They were supported by socialists and middle-class radicals.
                •	Attempted reforms:
                    •	Allowed Catalonia some autonomy.
                    •	Increased workers’ wages.
                    •	Nationalized large estates.
                    •	Separated the Church from the State.
                •	These reforms caused internal conflicts among the Right-Wing factions.
                •	Pro-Church CEDA party defended:
                    •	The Church.
                    •	The rich landlords.
        1.3 Rise of Fascism and the Road to Civil War
            •	1932: Army officers attempted a coup against Prime Minister Manuel Azaña, but it failed.
            •	Left-Wing (Communist-inspired) parties launched agitations to weaken the Republic.
            •	1933 elections:
                •	Azaña’s government was defeated.
                •	A Right-Wing government, dominated by the CEDA, was formed.
                •	The government failed to restore peace.
            •	Formation of the Fascist Party (Falange, 1933):
                •	Founded by José Antonio de Rivera, son of the former dictator.
                •	Supported by the Army.
            •	1936: The Army incited rebellion in Spanish Morocco, sparking the Spanish Civil War.
        1.4 The Spanish Civil War (1936–1939)
            •	Brutal conflict with atrocities on both sides.
            •	Became a proxy war for world powers.
            •	Key participants:
                •	Fascists (led by General Francisco Franco):
                    •	Received arms, troops, food, and ammunition from Hitler (Germany) and Mussolini (Italy).
                •	Republicans:
                    •	Direct support from the Soviet Union (Russia).
                    •	Unofficial support from Britain and France (sent “volunteers” to fight).
            •	March 1939: Franco captured Madrid and declared himself Dictator.
            •	Ruled Spain until 1975.
        1.5 Significance of the Spanish Civil War
            •	Though an internal conflict, it is often called a “Little World War” due to international involvement.
            •	Spain remained neutral in World War II under Franco’s rule.

    1. Britain and France in the Inter-War Period
        1.1 Britain in the Inter-War Years
            1.1.1 Economic Decline Post-World War I
                •	Britain emerged victorious from World War I but lost its position as the world’s foremost industrial power to the United States.
                •	The country suffered from economic stagnation, like the rest of Europe.
                •	To revive the economy, the government implemented:
                    •	Wage reductions to make British goods more competitive.
                    •	This backfired, leading to strikes and industrial unrest.
                •	Political impact:
                    •	The failure of economic policies ended Conservative supremacy.
                    •	The Labour Party came to power in 1924 and 1929.
                •	The Great Depression (1929) worsened economic conditions.
                •	1931: A National Government was formed with representatives from the Conservative, Liberal, and Labour parties.
            1.1.2 Social and Economic Challenges
                •	Women’s Suffrage (1918):
                    •	Representation of the People’s Act granted women the right to vote, but:
                        •	The voting age for women was 30, while for men, it was 21.
                •	Economic Problems:
                    •	1920: A slump in British overseas trade led to massive unemployment.
                    •	Labour Party demanded:
                        •	Reforming the industrial system along socialist lines.
                    •	Strikes in railways and coal industries shook the country.
            1.1.3 The Irish Problem (1918–1922)
                •	1918 elections:
                    •	The old Irish Nationalist Party was defeated.
                    •	Sinn Féin emerged as the dominant party.
                •	Sinn Féin leaders Michael Collins and Arthur Griffith:
                    •	Advocated Republicanism.
                    •	Set up a Republican government in Ireland.
                    •	Rejected British authority.
                •	British response:
                    •	Offered to establish two separate governments in Dublin and Belfast.
                    •	Brutally suppressed Irish resistance.
                    •	Led to severe public resentment in both Ireland and Britain.
                •	1921: Under pressure from the United States, the David Lloyd George government negotiated a settlement:
                    •	Irish Free State was created with British dominion status.
                    •	Accepted by Griffith and Collins, but rejected by Eamon de Valera.
                    •	1922: Irish Civil War erupted between:
                        •	Republicans.
                        •	Those who accepted the 1921 treaty.
            1.1.4 Britain’s Industrial and Economic Struggles
                •	Britain’s economic power, built in the 19th century, declined significantly.
                •	Before World War I, Britain dominated:
                    •	Textiles, heavy engineering, and manufactured goods.
                •	Post-war competition:
                    •	United States and Germany captured British markets.
                    •	Asian and African colonies started producing their own goods.
                    •	Tariff barriers in America and Europe made British exports uncompetitive.
                •	Great Depression impact (1929):
                    •	Britain faced a severe financial crisis.
                    •	The James Ramsay MacDonald government had to:
                        •	Raise taxes (a politically risky move).
                        •	Establish a National Government in 1931, including all major parties.
                •	Recovery Measures:
                    •	Increased taxes and wage cuts (unpopular but necessary).
                    •	Trade agreements with Canada and Australia boosted exports.
                    •	By 1934, the economy showed signs of recovery.
        1.2 France in the Inter-War Years
            1.2.1 Economic Challenges and Political Struggles
                •	France faced severe economic problems, worsened by:
                    •	The threat of Fascist and Communist movements.
                    •	The Bolshevik takeover in Russia (1917), which deprived France of an anti-German ally.
                •	French Military Interventions:
                    •	Sent troops to support anti-communist forces in Russia (White Russians).
                    •	1920: Helped Poland defeat Russia in the Polish-Soviet War.
                •	Rising international tensions:
                    •	1935: Britain signed the Anglo-German Naval Agreement, allowing Germany to build submarines (violating the Treaty of Versailles).
                    •	France felt betrayed and was unable to counter Hitler’s remilitarization of the Rhineland.
            1.2.2 Economic Development Amidst Class Struggles
                •	France’s economic condition improved during the inter-war years.
                •	However, class conflicts emerged, hindering national progress.
                •	Governments of Raymond Poincaré (1922–1924) and Édouard Herriot (1924–1926):
                    •	Industries prospered, but at the cost of labor rights.

    Rise of the United States
        1. The United States as an Economic Power
            •	By the late 19th century, the U.S. had already become a global economic power.
            •	After World War I, its vast industrial capacity and large domestic market reinforced its dominance in global trade.
            •	During World War I, the U.S. loaned the Allies over $12 billion at a 5% interest rate.
            •	In the 1920s, the U.S. government encouraged American corporations to expand profits in Europe, Canada, and Central and South America.
            •	The Republican Party (1921–1933) followed an isolationist policy, keeping the U.S. out of European politics.
            •	The U.S. did not join the League of Nations but could not fully disengage due to its global economic interests.
            •	The American public demanded repayment of loans given to Britain and France.
        2. Domestic Policies and Social Inequality
            •	Successive U.S. Presidents in the inter-war years ignored social reforms.
            •	Government policies prioritized big businesses over workers’ rights.
            •	When individual states passed progressive laws, the Supreme Court often struck them down.
            •	The economic boom benefited private industries but widened the gap between the rich and the poor.
            •	Labour rights were weak, and wages did not grow in proportion to economic expansion.
        3. U.S. Intervention in Germany’s Financial Crisis
            •	The U.S. played a key role in stabilizing Germany’s economy during its financial crisis in 1923.
            •	Under the Dawes Plan (1924) and Young Plan (1929), ‘German bonds’ were sold to American investors.
            •	This financial aid helped Germany pay reparations to Britain and France.
            •	Britain and France, in turn, used that money to repay their debts to the U.S.
        4. Economic Boom and the Great Depression
            •	The U.S. economy boomed in the 1920s due to strong industrial growth and financial capacity.
            •	American consumers created high demand for domestic goods.
            •	European products struggled to compete due to high tariff barriers protecting U.S. industries.
            •	By 1927, consumer demand peaked and then started to decline.
            •	The stock market became overextended, with inflated share prices and risky margin trading.
