            •	The Cold War remained “cold” with no direct conflict.
            •	Both sides feared accidental war, which could destroy humanity.
            •	Spies and pacts were common, but direct war was avoided.

    25. ORIGINS OF THE COLD WAR
        25.1 Strained Relations Before WWII
            •	Allied powers and the USSR had tense relations until Hitler’s invasion of Russia in 1941.
            •	The US sent significant war material and humanitarian supplies to the USSR under the Lend-Lease system, with repayment expected later.
            •	President Roosevelt of the US had a good relationship with Stalin, but Harry S. Truman (Roosevelt’s successor) was highly suspicious of Stalin’s intentions.
        25.2 Stalin’s Concerns
            •	Stalin feared that once Nazi Germany was defeated, the US and Britain would turn their war machinery against the USSR.
        25.3 The Yalta Conference
            •	In February 1945, Roosevelt, Stalin, and Winston Churchill met at Yalta to plan the post-WWII world.
            •	Germany was to be divided into three zones: British, American, and Soviet. The French zone was added later.
            •	Berlin, in the middle of Germany, was also divided into three zones (later four).
            •	The creation of the United Nations was agreed to replace the League of Nations.
            •	Democracies would be established in the countries freed from Nazi rule, allowing the people to elect their governments.
            •	The USSR would join the war against Japan, with the condition of receiving the Sakhalin Islands and some territory in Manchuria.
        25.4 The Potsdam Conference
            •	In July 1945, a follow-up conference was held at Potsdam between Churchill, Stalin, and Truman (Roosevelt had died in April 1945).
            •	The meeting revealed tensions between the three powers, and no decision could be reached on the long-term future of Germany.
            •	The biggest issue was Poland. Stalin had broken his promise by occupying a large part of Eastern Germany.
            •	A pro-communist regime was established in Poland, leading to atrocities and mass evictions of German people.

    26. Communist Satellite States
        26.1 Stalin’s Puppet Governments
            •	Stalin exacerbated the crisis by establishing puppet communist governments in Poland, Hungary, Bulgaria, Albania, and Romania.
            •	Political opponents were systematically eliminated in most of these countries.
            •	In Hungary, where Soviets allowed elections, the communists won less than 20% of the vote, but Stalin ordered mass murder and terrorized non-communists.
            •	By the end of 1947, every Eastern European country, except Czechoslovakia, had a Stalinist government.
        26.2 Yugoslavia’s Resistance
            •	Yugoslavia was an exception. Marshal Tito, who had led a heroic partisan struggle against the Nazis, became the President in 1945.
            •	Since the Soviets did not have an army stationed there, Stalin could not take action against Tito.
        26.3 Stalin’s Speech on Communism
            •	In February 1946, Stalin stated that communism and capitalism could never coexist peacefully.
            •	He declared that future wars were inevitable until the final victory of world communism.
        26.4 The Coup in Czechoslovakia
            •	In February 1948, Czechoslovakia—the last free Eastern European country—suffered a Moscow-backed coup and a complete communist takeover.
            •	The country had previously had a coalition government of communists and other left-wing parties after the 1946 elections, but the coup shocked the world.
        26.5 The Truman Doctrine
            •	In March 1947, President Truman announced the Truman Doctrine, stating that America would support free people resisting subjugation by armed minorities or outside pressures.
            •	The doctrine was spurred by the civil war in Greece, where communists began fighting post-war.
            •	It also committed the US to a policy of containing communism not only in Europe but globally.

    27. Marshall Plan and Cominform
        27.1 Post-War Economic Recovery
            •	World War II devastated the economies of Western Europe under US influence.
            •	In June 1947, George C. Marshall announced the European Recovery Programme, offering financial aid to rebuild Europe.
            •	The goal was to revive economies and open up markets, addressing poverty, which could breed communism.
        27.2 Exclusion of Eastern Europe
            •	The Marshall Plan was offered to Eastern Europe, but Soviet regimes couldn’t decide independently.
            •	The Soviets called it ‘dollar imperialism’ and blocked funds from reaching Eastern Europe.
        27.3 Stalin’s Response: Cominform
            •	In September 1947, Stalin set up Cominform to:
            •	Assert the supremacy of Soviet-style communism.
            •	Support industrialization of the East Bloc.
            •	Announce exclusive trade among East Bloc nations.
        27.4 Yugoslavia’s Expulsion
            •	Yugoslavia, opposing Stalin’s policy, was expelled from Cominform.
        27.5 Comecon and Molotov Plan
            •	Comecon coordinated the Eastern Bloc’s economic development.
            •	The Molotov Plan was the Soviet alternative to the Marshall Plan to finance Comecon.

    28. Berlin Blockade
        28.1 Crisis Overview
            •	The Berlin Blockade, starting in 1948, was the biggest crisis of the early Cold War.
            •	Stalin not only prevented the implementation of the Marshall Plan in Soviet-controlled Germany but also exploited the region.
            •	German civilians suffered from starvation and oppression under Soviet control.
            •	Meanwhile, the British, American, and French zones merged into a single economic unit and quickly began recovering.
            •	As a result, thousands of refugees fled westward.
        28.2 Soviet Actions
            •	The Soviets sealed all roads, rails, and canal links between West and East Berlin, cutting off western aid to the people in East Berlin.
            •	East Berliners faced starvation due to the blockade.
        28.3 Western Response
            •	The United States launched an airlift, flying in food and essential supplies, even at the risk of intruding into Soviet airspace.
            •	This airlift continued for 10 months without interruption.
        28.4 Conclusion
            •	The Soviets ultimately lifted the blockade in 1949, unable to prevent the ongoing supply effort.

    29. Formation of NATO
        29.1 Creation of NATO
            •	After their success, the western powers sought to create a strategic alliance to prevent a communist invasion of Western Europe backed by the USSR.
            •	The North Atlantic Treaty Organisation (NATO) was established in April 1949.
            •	A key principle of NATO was that an attack on any one of the 10 founding members would be regarded as an attack on all.
        29.2 Expansion and Response
            •	Over time, NATO grew into a formidable military bloc focused on defending the Western Hemisphere.
            •	In 1954, the Soviets created their own version of NATO, called the Warsaw Pact.
        29.3 Nuclear Arms Race
            •	After the United States became the first country with nuclear capability, their use of the atomic bomb on Hiroshima and Nagasaki in World War II was met with global condemnation.
            •	Stalin directed his secret agents worldwide to steal U.S. nuclear secrets, with the help of communist sympathizers.
            •	These efforts enabled the Soviets to obtain the blueprints for the atomic bomb, leading to a successful test in September 1949.
            •	This triggered the start of the nuclear arms race, a continual global threat to peace.
        29.4 U.S. Response and Global Arms Race
            •	In response, President Truman ordered the development and testing of a hydrogen bomb, much more powerful than an atomic bomb.
            •	This led to an ongoing arms race involving many nations, diverting resources away from development to military production.

    End of Stalin Era and the Rise of Khrushchev
        1. Death of Stalin and Leadership Transition
            •	Joseph Stalin died on 5 March 1953. New evidence suggests he may have been murdered by his close aides.
            •	His war-mongering nature had created anxiety even among the top ranks of the Soviet Communist Party.
            •	After his death, Georgi Malenkov and Nikita Khrushchev took over, showing inclination toward peace with the United States.
            •	In 1956, Khrushchev fundamentally altered Stalin’s worldview by advocating peaceful co-existence between the West and East, stating:
                •	“There are only two ways—either peaceful co-existence or the most destructive war in history. There is no third way.”
            •	Though Khrushchev remained committed to world communism, he did not believe in war as a means to achieve it.
        2. The ‘Thaw’ in Cold War Tensions
            •	The thaw in relations between the USSR and the West began under Khrushchev.
            •	He made key concessions, including:
                •	Withdrawing USSR’s military bases from Finland.
                •	Lifting the Soviet veto in the UN Security Council, allowing 16 new member states to join.
                •	Patching up relations with Yugoslavia.
                •	Formally abandoning Cominform.
        3. Soviet Control Over Satellite States
            •	Despite the thaw, Khrushchev had no intention of loosening control over Soviet satellite states.
            •	In 1956, a popular uprising erupted in Budapest, Hungary.
            •	Khrushchev responded by sending troops to brutally crush the rebellion, proving that the thaw was only superficial.
            •	To further solidify Soviet influence, he formed the Warsaw Pact (1955), a military alliance including:
                •	Albania, Bulgaria, Czechoslovakia, East Germany, Hungary, Poland, Romania, and the USSR.
                •	A unified military command was established in Moscow.
        4. The Arms Race and Space Race
            •	Khrushchev aggressively armed the USSR with advanced weapons.
            •	The Soviet Hydrogen Bomb was developed using espionage networks and communist sympathizers in the West.
            •	The USSR also developed:
                •	A long-range bomber plane capable of reaching the United States.
                •	The Intercontinental Ballistic Missile (ICBM), capable of striking U.S. cities with nuclear warheads.
            •	The U.S. responded by developing their own ICBM, the Atlas, escalating the arms race.
            •	The rivalry extended into space:
                •	The USSR launched Sputnik I (1958), the world’s first artificial satellite.
                •	The U.S. countered with Explorer 1.
        5. The Berlin Crisis and the Cold War’s Continuation
            •	By 1958, Khrushchev had gained a strategic edge in the arms race and used it to challenge the West.
            •	He declared that the USSR no longer recognized Western rights in West Berlin.
            •	The U.S. responded with a firm stance, warning against any Soviet aggression.
            •	In 1961, the Berlin Wall was built to stop East Germans from escaping to the West.
            •	The Berlin Wall stood for 29 years, symbolizing the division of Germany.
            •	Many people made daring escape attempts, but most were shot down by communist forces.

    The Cuban Missile Crisis (1962)
        1. Communist Takeover of Cuba
            •	In January 1959, Cuba came under communist rule when Fidel Castro’s guerrilla army overthrew the corrupt Batista regime.
            •	The United States had significant business interests in Cuba.
            •	Castro retaliated by nationalizing U.S. plantations and industries, causing thousands of Cubans to flee to the U.S. by boat.
        2. U.S. Response and the Bay of Pigs Invasion (1961)
            •	President Dwight D. Eisenhower adopted an aggressive stance toward Cuba.
            •	The CIA began training Cuban refugees in the U.S. to launch an attack and overthrow Castro’s communist government.
            •	President John F. Kennedy, Eisenhower’s successor, authorized the invasion.
            •	In April 1961, 1,400 men landed at the Bay of Pigs from the U.S. military base in Guantanamo Bay.
            •	The operation was poorly planned, and Castro’s forces easily crushed the invaders.
        3. Soviet Involvement and Missile Deployment
            •	The USSR provided economic assistance to Cuba to counter the impact of the U.S. blockade.
            •	In 1962, U.S. intelligence discovered that the Soviets had established missile bases inside Cuba.
            •	Satellite and aerial photographs confirmed the presence of nuclear missiles.
            •	This directly threatened the U.S., as Soviet missiles could strike American cities in the event of war.
            •	The USSR resumed atmospheric nuclear testing on 1 September 1961, while the U.S. responded with underground nuclear tests.
        4. The Crisis and Resolution
            •	The UN Secretary-General, U Thant, appealed for restraint from both sides.
            •	The crisis lasted for several tense days.
            •	Ultimately, Khrushchev agreed to remove Soviet missiles from Cuba in exchange for a U.S. pledge not to attack Cuba.
            •	President Kennedy also agreed to dismantle American missiles in Turkey as part of the deal.
            •	To prevent future misunderstandings, a direct telephone link, known as the ‘hotline’, was established between the U.S. and Soviet leaders for swift communication in times of crisis.

    Regional Military Alliances During the Cold War
        During the Cold War, nations formed military alliances to protect themselves against perceived threats from the superpowers—the United States and the Soviet Union. 
            These alliances were primarily politico-military pacts, with the North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) being the largest and most influential.
        1. U.S.-Led Military Alliances
            •	NATO (North Atlantic Treaty Organization) – The most significant military alliance, formed to counter the Soviet threat in Europe.
            •	US-Japan Mutual Security Treaty – A defense pact ensuring U.S. military protection for Japan.
            •	US-Korea Mutual Security Treaty – A similar agreement for South Korea’s security.
            •	ANZUS (Australia, New Zealand, and the U.S.) – A defense alliance among these three Pacific nations.
            •	SEATO (Southeast Asia Treaty Organization, 1954) – Modeled after NATO, this alliance included most of the Southeast Asian countries to counter communist expansion.
            •	CENTO (Central Treaty Organization, 1955) – A defense alliance involving Arab states, designed to block Soviet influence in the Middle East.
        2. Limitations of These Alliances
            Despite their strategic intent, these alliances often proved impractical in execution. 
                Many member states had conflicting interests, limiting their effectiveness in addressing regional security challenges.

    The Bandung Conference and the Non-Aligned Movement (NAM)
        1. Growing Concerns of the 1950s
            •	By the mid-1950s, the world was increasingly militarized and divided into two opposing camps.
            •	A devastating war seemed imminent.
            •	Poverty, inequality, and colonialism were being ignored.
            •	Leaders of Indonesia, Ceylon (Sri Lanka), and the Philippines initiated a conference to address these concerns.
        2. The Bandung Conference (1955)
            •	Held in Bandung, Indonesia, with representatives from 29 African and Asian nations.
            •	Aimed to promote cultural and economic cooperation and resist colonialism.
            •	Prominent leaders:
                •	Jawaharlal Nehru (India)
                •	Gamal Abdel Nasser (Egypt)
                •	Zhou Enlai (China)
                •	Ho Chi Minh (Vietnam)
                •	Kwame Nkrumah (Gold Coast, later Ghana)
            •	Excluded countries: South Africa, Israel, Taiwan, South Korea, and North Korea.
            •	Led to the formation of the Non-Aligned Movement (NAM) in 1961.
        3. The Non-Aligned Movement (NAM)
            •	Started with the goal of staying neutral during the Cold War.
            •	Played a moral role against the arms race and military expansion of superpowers.
            •	Passed resolutions condemning superpower actions.
            •	Actively opposed apartheid in South Africa.
        4. Challenges Faced by NAM
            •	Lacked a permanent secretariat.
            •	Was largely inactive between summit meetings.
            •	Failed to prevent conflicts among its own member states, such as the Iran-Iraq War.
            •	Could not act when the USSR invaded Afghanistan in 1979.
            •	Many member states depended on superpowers for economic support.
        5. NAM After the Cold War
            •	After the Cold War ended, many questioned its relevance.
            •	SEATO and CENTO had already dissolved, but some members wanted to retain NAM.
            •	New concern: Unipolarism (US dominance as the world’s only superpower).
            •	Despite reaffirming its goals, NAM failed to take action during the 2003 Iraq crisis.

    Nuclear Arms Control and Disarmament
        1. The Nuclear Test Ban Treaty (1963)
            •	Signed by the US, USSR, and Britain.
            •	Marked the beginning of détente (easing of tensions).
            •	Banned nuclear tests in the atmosphere, underwater, and outer space.
        2. Shift Towards Arms Control
            •	By the 1960s, both superpowers realized that the arms race was endless.
            •	Research and development ensured that one power would always catch up with the other.
            •	They began signing treaties to limit research, testing, and deployment of nuclear weapons.
        3. The Outer Space Treaty (1967)
            •	Signed by the US, USSR, and 60 other countries.
            •	Declared outer space a zone of peace.
        4. The Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty (NPT) (1970)
            •	Signed by the US, USSR, and 58 other nations.
            •	Aimed to prevent the spread of nuclear weapons.
            •	Key provisions:
                •	US and USSR agreed not to supply nuclear weapons or technology to other countries.
                •	Banned nuclear testing by countries not capable of building nuclear weapons.
            •	India opposed the NPT, calling it discriminatory and insisting on universal disarmament.
        5. Strategic Arms Limitation Treaty (SALT-I) (1972)
            •	Signed on 26 May 1972.
            •	Restricted the development of:
                •	Anti-Ballistic Missiles (ABMs)
                •	Intercontinental Ballistic Missiles (ICBMs)
                •	Submarine-Launched Ballistic Missiles (SLBMs)
            •	Introduced the Mutually Assured Destruction (MAD) doctrine, ensuring that neither power had an advantage.
        6. SALT-II Talks (1979)
            •	Initiated by US President Jimmy Carter and Soviet leader Leonid Brezhnev.
            •	Aimed to further limit long-range nuclear missiles.
            •	Talks collapsed after the USSR invaded Afghanistan in 1979.
            •	NATO escalated tensions by announcing Dual Track deployment in Europe to counter Warsaw Pact missiles.
        7. The Strategic Arms Reduction Treaty (START) (1982-1983)
            •	Proposed by US President Ronald Reagan.
            •	Introduced the Zero Option, aiming to eliminate an entire class of nuclear missiles.
            •	Suggested reducing ICBMs and signing a verifiable arms control agreement.
            •	Talks broke down in 1983 due to Reagan’s insistence on the Strategic Defense Initiative (SDI), or “Star Wars”.
            •	SDI aimed to intercept enemy missiles from space, leading to fears of a new arms race in outer space.
        8. Breakthrough with Mikhail Gorbachev (1985-1991)
            •	Gorbachev revived START negotiations with new proposals.
            •	At the Geneva Summit (1985), Reagan and Gorbachev agreed to reduce nuclear arms by 50%.
            •	Nuclear Risk Reduction Centre Agreement (1987) improved communication and trust.
            •	Treaty of Conventional Armed Forces in Europe (1990) reduced land army strength.
            •	START Treaty signed on 31 July 1991, just five months before the USSR dissolved.
        9. Post-Cold War Nuclear Disarmament
            •	The Cold War ended, but the START Treaty remains in effect.
            •	Russia inherited the USSR’s international obligations.
            •	Both Russia and the US continue to uphold the treaty’s nuclear disarmament commitments.

    Socio-Economic Impact of the Cold War
        1. Global Economic Growth Amidst Uncertainty
            •	The Cold War era saw rapid industrialization and global economic expansion.
            •	However, constant regional conflicts created uncertainty, with fears of a potential World War III.
        2. Economic Revival in the Western World
            •	Superpowers used financial resources to gain the loyalty of allies.
            •	This accelerated economic recovery in Western Europe.
            •	After centuries of war, Western European nations realized the need for economic and social cooperation.
        3. Early Steps Toward European Economic Unity
            •	Winston Churchill (1943) proposed a Council of Europe and a “United States of Europe”.
            •	1948: The Organisation of European Economic Cooperation (OEEC) was established, doubling trade volumes within its first six years.
            •	The International Trade Organisation and GATT (General Agreement on Trade and Tariffs) were formed to reduce import duties and promote free global trade.
        4. Formation of the European Economic Community (EEC) (1957)
            •	The most significant Cold War economic bloc.
            •	Established by France, West Germany, Italy, Netherlands, Belgium, and Luxembourg under the Treaty of Rome.
            •	Inspired by the European Coal and Steel Community (1951-56), which boosted European steel production by 50%.
            •	EEC’s goals:
                •	Remove customs duties and trade quotas.
                •	Encourage free competition among member states.
            •	Success of the EEC (by 1961):
                •	Reflected in economic growth statistics.
                •	Led Britain to join in 1973, despite initially staying away.
        5. Struggles of the Communist Bloc
            •	While Western Europe prospered, the Eastern Bloc economies struggled.
            •	Khrushchev’s attempt to unify Eastern Bloc economies under COMECON failed due to internal resistance.
            •	Though economic institutions remained weak, the Communist bloc had a strong record in social services.


- Asia in ferment
    Asia’s Transformation in the 20th Century
        •	The 21st century is often anticipated as the ‘Asian Century’, potentially bringing extraordinary progress for one-third of humanity.
        •	However, in the 20th century, Asia started at a major disadvantage compared to Europe and the Americas.
        •	The second half of the 20th century saw widespread turmoil across Asia:
            •	Violent revolutions
            •	Mass movements
            •	Social upheavals
            •	Economic struggles followed by recovery in some cases
        •	To understand this transformation, we must begin with the Chinese Communist Revolution, which serves as the starting point of Asia’s dramatic evolution.

    China
        The Chinese Communist Revolution and Mao’s Reforms
            1. Establishment of Communist Rule (1949)
                •	The Chinese Communist Party (CCP), led by Mao Zedong, gained control over mainland China in 1949 after a long and violent civil war.
                •	At the time, China was devastated due to:
                    •	Years of civil war
                    •	Japanese occupation
                    •	Severe food shortages
                    •	Underdeveloped agricultural infrastructure
                    •	Minimal industrial base
                •	Despite extreme poverty, the people placed their faith in Mao and the Communists.
                •	Mao adopted Marxist ideology, blending it with Chinese traditions, tailoring reforms to China’s unique challenges.
            2. A Peasant-Led Revolution
                •	The Chinese Communist Revolution was the world’s first large-scale peasant revolution, unlike the Russian Revolution, which was led by industrial workers.
                •	Mao’s strategy was to build a coalition of:
                    •	Peasants
                    •	Workers
                    •	Petty bourgeoisie
                    •	National bourgeoisie (small business owners and industrialists)
                •	Mao initially allowed businessmen to retain their properties if they supported the revolution, but by 1956, the state took over all private lands and businesses.
            3. Industrialisation and Economic Growth
                •	Industrialisation was given high priority:
                    •	Oil production made China one of the world’s largest producers by 1977.
                    •	China ranked third in coal production and led in various minerals.
                    •	Biogas plants were set up to produce methane gas and fertiliser from waste.
                    •	By 1960, China’s steel production rivaled France, and power generation increased tenfold.
                    •	Irrigated land doubled (1949–1960).
                    •	Large-scale afforestation projects were launched.
                    •	The railway network was expanded, enabling industrial dispersal beyond Manchuria and the East.
                •	Small-scale industries thrived, aided by Soviet assistance in:
                    •	Precision tools
                    •	Electronics
                    •	Daily-use goods (scissors, pens, toys, surgical instruments, switches, etc.)
                •	‘Made in China’ products gained credibility in international markets due to their high quality and low cost.
            4. Land Reforms and Collectivisation
                •	Land reforms began by confiscating large landholdings from landlords.
                •	Cooperative farming was introduced, where multiple villages pooled resources.
                •	By 1957, more than 90% of agricultural land was collectivised.
                •	In the next phase, state-controlled collective farms replaced cooperatives, ensuring direct Communist Party oversight.
            5. The ‘Great Leap Forward’ (1959) and Its Failure
                •	Mao launched the ‘Great Leap Forward’ to accelerate industrialisation and agricultural production.
                •	Key measures:
                    •	Rural cooperatives were merged into large communes, symbolizing state ownership over local control.
                    •	A mobile labor force was created to support industrial projects.
                •	Results:
                    •	The plan failed catastrophically due to incompetent Communist Party officials managing it.
                    •	Food shortages worsened, and industrial production collapsed.
                •	In 1962, the strategy was revised:
                    •	Agriculture was prioritized.
                    •	Communes were downsized.
                    •	Farmers were allowed to cultivate small private plots.
        The Great Chinese Famine
            1. The Promise vs. Reality
                •	Mao’s ‘Great Leap Forward’ was launched with the slogan:
                    •	“Three years of hard work and suffering, and a thousand years of prosperity.”
                •	However, instead of prosperity, China faced an unprecedented famine.
                •	Initially, everything appeared successful as:
                    •	Communist Party units reported exaggerated agricultural output.
                    •	Encouraged by false reports, the government set even higher production targets.
            2. Technical Failures and Agricultural Mismanagement
                •	The primary reasons for failure were flawed agricultural techniques:
                    •	Seeds were sown at ten times the normal density, suffocating young plants.
                    •	Wheat and maize were grown on the same fields, which proved ineffective.
                    •	Mass extermination of sparrows (to prevent seed loss) led to uncontrolled pest infestations.
                    •	Poorly planned irrigation projects caused massive soil erosion.
                    •	Focus on a few select crops resulted in neglect of essential food crops and vegetables.
            3. Official Denial and Propaganda
                •	Chinese leaders believed in false production figures, leading to severe miscalculations.
                •	Despite famine, the Party continued propaganda, creating misleading slogans:
                    •	In 1959, when people were starving and boiling grass for food, they were advised to:
                        •	“Live frugally in a year of plenty.”
                    •	The state-run radio glorified starvation, urging citizens to:
                        •	“Take naps” since they were too weak to work.
                •	Doctors were instructed to convince people that Chinese physiology required neither proteins nor fats.
            4. Human Cost and Economic Collapse
                •	The 1960 harvest was 26% lower than 1957 levels.
                •	By 1960, food production had fallen to 1950 levels, but China’s population had increased by over 100 million.
                •	Consequences:
                    •	Rice prices skyrocketed—rising 30 times in some provinces.
                    •	Coal shortages disrupted emergency rail transport of food.
                    •	Miners abandoned work to search for food, worsening coal production.
                    •	Mortality rate surged from 15% to 68% in 1960.
                    •	Birth rate plummeted from 30% to 11%.
                •	The famine remained largely unknown to the outside world as China tightly controlled information.
            The Great Chinese Famine stands as one of the deadliest man-made disasters in history, caused by a combination of faulty policies, misinformation, and rigid state control.
        The Cultural Revolution
            1. Background and Mao’s Motivation
                •	The failure of the ‘Great Leap Forward’ weakened Mao’s influence.
                •	To reassert his control, he launched the Cultural Revolution, one of the darkest periods in Chinese communism.
                •	Mao, a hardline communist, feared that:
                    •	Party officials were becoming complacent.
                    •	Communist ideology was losing ground.
                    •	Resentment was growing due to the failures of the ‘Great Leap Forward’.
                •	Right-wing party leaders like Deng Xiaoping and Liu Shaoqi suggested reforms:
                    •	Higher wages for quality labor.
                    •	Allowing larger private holdings.
                    •	Guiding the revolution with expert managerial oversight rather than party cadres.
                •	Mao rejected these ideas, considering them a betrayal of Marxist-Leninist principles.
            2. The Launch of the Cultural Revolution
                •	In 1966, Mao announced the ‘Proletarian Cultural Revolution’, aiming to mobilize the youth to “save” the revolution.
                •	The Red Guards, composed mostly of students, became his strongest supporters.
                •	They traveled across China, carrying Mao’s ‘Little Red Book’, and:
                    •	Led massive propaganda campaigns to reinforce communist ideology.
                    •	Denounced and physically attacked ‘counter-revolutionaries’.
                    •	Targeted teachers, intellectuals, professionals, and local party officials.
                •	However, the movement soon spiraled out of control, leading to widespread chaos and violence.
        Anarchic Totalitarianism (1966-1976)
            1. The Death Toll and Lingering Famine Effects
                •	Unlike the Great Leap Forward, where millions died from famine, the Cultural Revolution resulted in:
                    •	400,000 to 1 million deaths, according to general estimates.
                    •	Some scholars, like Domenach, estimate 1 million to 3 million deaths.
                •	The effects of the Great Leap Forward famine were still being felt, making it difficult to determine which policy caused more devastation.
            2. The Red Guards’ Reign of Terror
                •	By 1967, daily executions were widespread.
                •	Mao encouraged the youth to embark on their own “Long March”, claiming they would “learn revolution by making revolution”.
                •	Schools shut down as students joined the Red Guards, often to save themselves from persecution.
                •	The Red Guards turned against their own families and communities:
                    •	Teenagers denounced their parents, teachers, and relatives.
                    •	Ancient monuments were destroyed, private homes raided, and random killings became common.
                    •	The movement developed its own armed investigators, parallel police forces, and judiciary, complete with judges and prisons.
            3. Chaos and Civil War Threat
                •	The Cultural Revolution soon spiraled into anarchy, pushing China to the brink of civil war.
                •	Strikes and protests erupted against the Cultural Revolution.
                •	China became isolated internationally, with even the communist bloc distancing itself.
                •	Mao reversed his stance and called upon the People’s Liberation Army (PLA) to suppress the Red Guards.
                •	Lin Biao, a key architect of the Cultural Revolution, was removed from power and died in a mysterious air crash in 1971.
            4. The Second Phase of the Cultural Revolution
                •	The Red Guards turned against each other.
                    •	Students attacked workers.
                    •	Workers targeted day laborers.
                •	Industrial production collapsed, and civil society disappeared, replaced by violence, slogans, and political campaigns.
                •	Mao continued purging his close allies, even ordering the mysterious execution of Lin Biao, whom he had once named as his successor.
                •	Disillusionment grew, and when Mao died in 1976, the nation heaved a sigh of relief.
                •	The ‘Gang of Four’, led by Mao’s wife Jiang Qing, was jailed, reflecting the public’s deep resentment toward Mao’s rule.
        The US-China Rapprochement (1972)
            1. Nixon’s Historic Visit to China
                •	In February 1972, the world was shocked to learn that US President Richard Nixon would visit Beijing.
                •	This was unprecedented, as US-China relations had been severed since the Korean War.
            2. ‘Ping Pong’ Diplomacy: The Secret US-China Talks
                •	‘Ping Pong’ diplomacy was the term for clandestine meetings between US and Chinese officials from the late 1960s to early 1970s.
                •	Since the US had not formally recognized Communist China, it could not engage with its officials openly.
                •	Meanwhile, China’s relations with the USSR had deteriorated due to:
                    •	Ideological differences.
                    •	Leadership disputes over the world communist movement.
                    •	Border conflicts between China and the USSR.
                •	The US sought to exploit this Sino-Soviet rift to counter Soviet influence.
                •	Pakistan, an ally of both the US and China, acted as a facilitator in these secret negotiations.
            3. Outcomes of Nixon’s Visit
                •	The visit led to the US officially recognizing the People’s Republic of China (PRC) as the legitimate government of China.
                •	Taiwan was sidelined:
                    •	It lost its seat in the UN Security Council to Communist China.
                    •	However, it continued to receive American aid.
                •	The visit paved the way for formal US-China diplomatic relations, reshaping global geopolitics.
        Deng Xiaoping’s Rise and Reforms
            •	After Mao’s death, a power struggle followed.
            •	Deng Xiaoping emerged victorious in 1979.
            •	He had been removed during the Cultural Revolution for being “too liberal.”
            •	Once in power, he reversed many policies of that era.
            Political and Social Reforms
                •	Revolutionary committees were replaced with democratically elected groups.
                •	Confiscated property was returned to survivors of original owners.
                •	Religious freedom was restored.
                •	Intellectuals were given more freedom of expression.
            Economic Reforms and Modernization
                •	Deng and Hu Yaobang sought Western investment.
                •	China joined the World Bank and IMF in 1980.
                •	State farms gained more control over administration and planning.
                •	Profit-sharing and bonuses were encouraged.
                •	Tax reductions boosted agricultural output.
                •	Grain production increased, improving nutrition levels.
            Impact
                •	Deng’s message: “To get rich is not a crime.”
                •	The Party reduced its role in the economy.
                •	By the late 20th century, per capita income rose to $1,200.
                •	China transformed into a modern industrial state.
        Democracy Wall Movement and Deng’s Response
            •	November 1978: People in Beijing and other cities began protesting for more reforms.
            •	The government banned marches, leading people to create “democracy walls.”
            •	Anonymous posters and graffiti demanded human rights and criticized Mao.
            Deng’s Reaction
                •	Deng, a party loyalist, opposed uncontrolled dissent.
                •	He believed China would fall into chaos without the Party.
                •	Democracy walls were banned, and leaders of the movement were arrested and sentenced.
                •	However, Mao-era terror did not return under Deng.
        Tiananmen Square Protests and Government Crackdown
            •	Party Hardliners vs. Reformists:
                •	Deng faced pressure from hardliners like Li Peng, who wanted his reforms to fail.
                •	He promoted liberal leaders like Zhao Ziyang and Hu Yaobang but kept criticism within limits.
            •	Economic Issues (1988-89):
                •	Inflation rose, and wages lagged behind prices.
                •	Students in Beijing began peaceful protests in Tiananmen Square (May 1989).
                •	They demanded political reforms, democracy, and an end to corruption.
            •	The Protest and Government Response:
                •	Zhao assured them their demands would be addressed.
                •	The demonstrations were festive, with music, flags, and a ‘Democracy’ statue.
                •	The world press covered the events.
            •	The Crackdown (June 3-4, 1989):
                •	The government saw it as a threat to Communist rule.
                •	Deng turned hardliner, replaced Zhao with Li Peng, and sent in the Army.
                •	Tanks rolled in, and 1,500–3,000 people were killed.
                •	The world was outraged, but China refused to repent.
                •	Deng justified the massacre, claiming one-party control was necessary for a ‘socialist market economy.’

    Tibet and Sino-Indian Relations
        •	Tibet’s Strategic Importance:
            •	Tibet, a high-altitude region, was strategically important for British India.
            •	China had ruled Tibet since the 18th century, while the British secretly mapped the region.
            •	In 1911, China granted Tibet independence.
            •	The Dalai Lama became both spiritual and political head.
        •	China’s Takeover of Tibet (1951):
            •	After British India’s exit, diplomatic ties between New Delhi and Lhasa continued.
            •	In 1951, China sponsored a communist uprising and seized Tibet.
            •	The Dalai Lama retained nominal control, but Buddhism was suppressed, causing tension.
        •	Tibetan Revolt and Dalai Lama’s Exile (1956-1959):
            •	Ethnic Tibetans in China revolted in 1956 against the persecution of religious leaders.
            •	The movement spread to Tibet, and in 1959, China crushed it with brute force.
            •	Hundreds were massacred, and sacred monuments were destroyed.
            •	Facing persecution, Dalai Lama fled to India with 100,000 followers.
            •	Nehru granted them refuge in Dalhousie, Himachal Pradesh.
        •	Sino-Indian Conflict (1962):
            •	China refused to recognise British-drawn borders.
            •	Though Nehru and Zhou Enlai agreed on Panchsheel (Five Principles), China’s People’s Liberation Army (PLA) frequently intruded into Indian territory.
            •	In November 1962, China launched a full-scale invasion.
            •	The ill-equipped Indian Army struggled, and China occupied large territories.
            •	Global condemnation followed, and the US provided military aid to India.
            •	China withdrew partially but retained over 50,000 sq. km of Indian land.
            •	In 1963, Pakistan illegally transferred part of Kashmir to China.
        •	Diplomatic Standoff and Normalization (1962-1988):
            •	India downgraded diplomatic ties with China from 1962 to 1979.
            •	In 1979, Atal Bihari Vajpayee visited Beijing, initiating dialogue.
            •	In 1988, PM Rajiv Gandhi visited China, restarting the normalization process.
            •	A Joint Working Group was formed to resolve the border dispute, but no progress was made.
        •	Present-Day Sino-Indian Relations:
            •	Trade relations have grown significantly.
            •	However, China is still seen as supporting Pakistan’s hostile actions.
            •	China refuses to acknowledge Sikkim as part of India.

    Vietnam’s Struggles and Wars (Post-World War II)
        1. War Against French Colonial Rule (1946-1954)
            •	Vietnam, the largest country in former French Indo-China, went through three major wars after World War II.
            •	After Japan’s surrender (1945), the French attempted to reclaim Vietnam.
            •	Ho Chi Minh’s Viet Minh (League for the Independence of Vietnam) had gained military experience fighting the Japanese.
            •	With the Japanese gone, Ho established the Democratic Republic of Vietnam.
            •	However, non-communist nationalists and religious leaders opposed his rule.
            •	In 1946, the French launched an attack to reclaim Vietnam, installing a puppet regime in 1948.
            •	Battle of Dien Bien Phu (1954):
                •	The French were completely defeated.
                •	Geneva Conference (1954): Vietnam was divided into communist North Vietnam and non-communist South Vietnam.
        2. The Vietnam War (1955-1975)
            •	Initially, the US urged France to negotiate with Ho Chi Minh and nationalist leader Diem Ngo Dinh.
            •	But the rise of Communist China and the Korean War changed US policy.
            •	President Eisenhower backed France to prevent Ho’s communist rule in Vietnam.
            •	After the French withdrew, the US supported the Diem regime in South Vietnam.
            Escalation of Conflict
                •	From 1959, tensions between North and South Vietnam escalated.
                •	Diem’s Unpopularity:
                •	He persecuted his political rivals, leading some to resort to guerrilla warfare.
                •	Another perspective suggests that North Vietnam sponsored the insurgency.
                •	A full-scale war erupted:
                •	North Vietnam was backed by USSR and China.
                •	US openly supported South Vietnam.
            US Involvement and Defeat
                •	Initially, the US sent military advisers to assist South Vietnam.
                •	By 1963, US troops landed in Vietnam, and by 1969, over 500,000 American soldiers were involved.
                •	Guerrilla Warfare Challenges:
                    •	Despite technological superiority, the US could not match North Vietnam’s fighting skills.
                    •	The Vietnamese used jungle warfare, drawing Americans into unfamiliar terrain.
                •	War Crimes and Public Backlash:
                    •	US troops committed massacres, making the war unpopular.
                    •	Example: My Lai Massacre (1967) – innocent civilians were slaughtered.
                    •	American public opinion turned against the war.
            End of the Vietnam War
                •	President Nixon (1971) decided the US could not achieve victory.
                •	The US had lost over 50,000 soldiers, and public opposition was strong.
                •	Paris Peace Talks led to a ceasefire agreement (January 1973).
                •	US troops withdrew by March 1973, but North-South fighting continued.
                •	In 1975, South Vietnam was defeated, and Vietnam was reunified.
        3. Post-War Conflicts (1975-1989)
            War with Cambodia (1978-1989)
                •	Cambodia was ruled by the Khmer Rouge (Communist regime) after a bloody civil war (1975).
                •	Khmer Rouge massacred over 1 million Cambodians in forced labor camps.
                •	Border clashes with Vietnam led to a Vietnamese invasion (1978).
                •	The Vietnamese Army occupied much of Cambodia, forcing Khmer Rouge into the jungles near Thailand.
            Conflict with China (1979)
                •	China supported Khmer Rouge leader Pol Pot.
                •	In 1979, China invaded Vietnam, but Vietnamese troops repelled the attack.
                •	China retreated but continued supporting Pol Pot.
            Vietnam’s Withdrawal from Cambodia (1989)
                •	Under UN intervention, Vietnam withdrew from Cambodia in 1989.
                •	Cambodia transitioned to democracy, and Vietnam focused on economic rebuilding.

    The Partition and War in Korea
        1. Partition of Korea (Post-World War II)
            •	Korea was under Japanese rule from 1910 to 1945.
            •	After World War II, the country was partitioned into communist North Korea and non-communist South Korea.
            •	At the time of Japan’s surrender,
                •	The US occupied the South (below the 38th Parallel).
                •	The Soviet Army controlled the North.
            •	The US backed an anti-communist government under Syngman Rhee in South Korea.
            •	The Soviets supported a communist regime under Kim Il Sung in North Korea.
        2. Rising Tensions and the Outbreak of War (1950-1953)
            •	1948: The Soviets withdrew from North Korea.
            •	1949: The Americans left South Korea.
            •	However, hostility remained, with:
                •	Large-scale refugee crossings.
                •	Armed incursions from both sides.
            •	25 June 1950: North Korea invaded South Korea, sparking a three-year war.
        3. The Cold War Context and US Involvement
            •	The Cold War between the US and the USSR had begun in Europe.
            •	February 1950: The Sino-Soviet Alliance was signed.
            •	It remains uncertain whether North Korea was encouraged by China or the USSR to start the war.
            •	The US saw the conflict as part of a broader communist expansion.
            •	America intervened, fearing that Soviet and Chinese influence would spread across Asia.
            •	US troops, backed by the UN, were sent from Japan to Korea.
        4. Major Phases of the Korean War
            •	The war saw multiple shifts in control:
                1.	UN Commander General Douglas MacArthur led an amphibious landing at Inchon, pushing North Korean forces beyond the 38th Parallel.
                2.	UN forces crossed the Yalu River, nearing China’s border.
                3.	China retaliated, sending troops to aid North Korea, pushing UN forces back.
                4.	Three years of stalemate followed.
            •	27 July 1953: An armistice was signed, but no peace treaty was ever concluded.
        5. Post-War Korea: A Divided Nation
            •	The Korean border remains closed.
            •	Relations between North and South Korea remain tense even after six decades.
            •	The issue of Korean unification remains unresolved.
        Post-War Reconstruction and Economic Growth in South Korea
            1. Challenges After the Korean War
                •	South Korea faced major obstacles in rebuilding its society and economy after the war.
                •	While two-thirds of the peninsula’s population resided in the South, most industrial infrastructure was located in the North.
                •	With almost no industrial base, South Korea had to start from scratch, focusing on:
                    •	Power plants
                    •	Agriculture
                    •	Basic industries
                •	US economic assistance in the 1950s was crucial in helping South Korea feed its population and rebuild its economy.
            2. Economic Development and the Role of the State
                •	South Korea embraced private ownership and capitalism.
                •	Since the 1960s, a government-business partnership coordinated a successful growth strategy based on:
                    •	Foreign investment
                    •	Export-driven industries
                    •	Sustained economic planning
                •	Per capita income rose dramatically from around $100 in the late 1950s to nearly $2,000.
                •	The rapid economic transformation was not a mere “miracle”, but the result of:
                    •	Effective government planning
                    •	Strategic use of foreign capital
                    •	A disciplined and highly educated workforce
                    •	Strong collaboration between political leaders and private entrepreneurs
            3. Social and Economic Inequality
                •	Despite economic growth, income distribution remained uneven.
                •	Workers’ wages did not keep pace with the economy’s rapid expansion.
                •	The government restricted labor unions and strikes, limiting workers’ ability to demand better conditions.
                •	Many believed that only certain social groups benefited from the economic boom.
                •	Even the new urban middle class, which gained from economic success, began demanding greater political freedoms.
            4. Shifting Priorities: Social and Political Changes
                •	As South Korea moved beyond rapid industrialization, new challenges emerged:
                    •	Equity and income distribution
                    •	Increased social spending
                    •	Economic liberalization (reducing government controls and fostering free markets)
                    •	Democratic reforms to create a more open political system
                •	The government had to shift from a “growth at all costs” mindset to a more balanced approach, addressing both economic and social concerns.

    Cambodia’s (Kampuchea) Path to Turmoil: From Independence to Civil War
        1. Independence and Neutrality (1953–1969)
            •	Cambodia gained independence from French Indo-China along with Vietnam in 1953.
            •	Under King Norodom Sihanouk, Cambodia maintained neutrality during regional conflicts in the 1950s.
            •	The rise of communism in Cambodia was seen as a threat by the US, which was stationed in neighboring South Vietnam.
            •	Tensions with the US increased when South Vietnamese forces pursued Vietnamese communist guerrillas into Cambodian territory.
            •	In response, Cambodia severed ties with the US.
            •	However, when evidence emerged that Ho Chi Minh was secretly supporting Cambodian communists, Sihanouk restored relations with the US in 1969.
        2. Political Upheaval and Civil War (1970–1975)
            •	In 1970, pro-American Premier Lon Nol overthrew King Sihanouk and abolished the monarchy.
            •	Lon Nol demanded that North Vietnam withdraw its 40,000 soldiers who had established bases in Cambodia.
            •	Sihanouk fled to China, where he later aligned with the communist Khmer Rouge.
            •	A civil war broke out between:
                •	The Vietnam-backed Khmer Rouge (communist insurgents)
                •	The US-backed government of Lon Nol
        3. Khmer Rouge Rule and Mass Atrocities (1975–1979)
            •	In April 1975, the Khmer Rouge captured Phnom Penh, Cambodia’s capital.
            •	Pol Pot, the group’s leader, enforced a radical vision of agrarian communism, believing cities and their inhabitants were undesirable.
            •	Hundreds of thousands of city dwellers were forcibly relocated to rural areas for:
                •	Agriculture
                •	Forest clearing
                •	Hard labor
            •	The country’s name was changed to Kampuchea.
            •	Mass atrocities followed, leading to an estimated two million deaths over the next four years due to:
                •	Execution
                •	Forced labor
                •	Starvation and disease
            •	The Khmer Rouge turned the entire country into a minefield to prevent refugees from escaping into camps set up in Thailand.
        4. Vietnamese Invasion and Continued Conflict (1978–1980s)
            •	In 1978, border conflicts with Vietnam escalated into a full-scale invasion in January 1979.
            •	The Vietnamese installed a Hanoi-backed government in Kampuchea.
            •	However, fearing that one communist regime had simply replaced another, thousands of Cambodians fled to Thailand.
            •	Starvation and hardship were widespread across the country.
            •	The Khmer Rouge retreated into the western jungles, using guerrilla warfare to continue their resistance.


- Post-war shakeup in Africa and Asia

    In a previous Chapter we have noted how the countries of Asia and Africa achieved their independence from coloriial rule in the aftermath of the World War II. But independence is not an end in itself. Preserving it through fulfilling the needs and aspirations of the people and forming the basis of a civil society on which can grow peace and stability is the challenge before .every free nation. Insurmountable problems seized the leaders of the new states of Africa after liberation. In this Chapter we shall discuss the early careers of some of the countries of Africa which have been in news over the past few decades. We shall also try to form an understanding of the collective potential of the new republics of Central Asia which emerged as independent entities after the collapse of the Soviet Union. They represent tremendous challenges before world security, and because they lie close to India, their developments are crucial for the stability of our own region.

    THe New Africa
        It will be an understatement to say that all the newly freed African nations began with tremendous handicaps. Each of them is a polyglot of several tribes who were earlier held together through brute force by the colonial masters of the respective countries. During their brief freedom struggles, they had somehow maintained a veneer of unity. But once the Europeans left, their people were caught in confusion as to which loyalty was greater — tribal or national. In Nigeria, Congo (Zaire), Burundi and Rwanda, tribal rivalry led to open civil war.

        We saw earlier that most of today's modern African states are not natural nations but were formed as a result of the partition of the continent prompted by the colonial interests. After independence each of them discovered that their economic well-being depended on export earnings, and that too from one or two agricultural products or natural resources. Nigeria, for instance, was totally dependent on the export of oil, Ghana on cocoa and Kenya on tea to name a few. Lacking economic diversity and an educated human resource base many of the independent African nations became victims of neo-colonialism, as their former imperial rulers still controlled their external outlook. Sovereignty was often diluted when other countries did not approve of some of their governments.

    Ghana
        This former Gold Coast republic had Kwame Nkrumah holding office as its first President between 1957 and 1966, when he was ousted through a military coup. Under his socialist government, Ghana's population became better off than most of their other African counterparts. He developed the country's principal export crop, cocoa, and also tried to diversify the economic base by promoting forestry, fishing. cattle-breeding, gold and bauxite mining. Nkrumah was an internationally known leader for promoting the concept of pan-Africanism - as he realised the imperative need to form a federation of African states through economic unions with Guinea and Mali. He was | also one of the promoters of the Organisation of African Unity (OAU) which was set up in 1963. But one of the flaws of his administration was overcentralisation of authority. He gradually discarded the Parliamentary system and introduced one-party rule which made him a sort of dictator. Repressive steps were initiated against political opponents. In 1959, an order was passed by which opponents could be expelled from the country and imprisoned up to five years in jail without trial. In 1964, all political parties were banned and Nkrumah promoted a strong personality cult around himself. This angered the country’s military leadership. In 1966, when Nkrumah was on a tour of China, a coup was staged. The military promised the nation a return to democracy and a new Constitution with safeguards against dictatorship. By 1969, the new Constitution was ready and fresh elections were held. Kofi Busia, head of the Progressive Party, became the Prime Minister.

        Busia, a former Oxford don, found that the Army was virtually controlling everything in Ghana. But the country's economic condition futher deteriorated under him. Ghana was caught in debt trap which necessitated devaluation of the currency. Tribal rivalries too surfaced during his term. Consequnently, in 1972 he was deposed by the Army which put Colonel Ignatius Acheampong in power. But the country's economic crisis was too deep to be resolved by a corrupt government. In July 1978, the Army removed him too on grounds of corruption and installed General Fred Akuffo in his place. But in June 1978, a junior Air Force officer of mixed Ghanian-Scottish parentage, Jerry Rawlings, staged another coup promising to weed out corruption and return the country under democratic rule. In July, elections were held and Hilla Limann was sworn in as the President. But nothing could be done to stem the economic rot that had set in Ghana. There was widespread unemployment and indusirial strikes broke out. Rawlings now staged his second coup to get rid of Limann. He then made himself Chairman of the Provisional National Defence Council (PNDC).

        Rawlings was a rare kind of military man, He did not want the Army to run the country, but merely have a say in the national decision-making process. He appointed a civilian government under the PNDC comprising well- known leaders and academicians. An economic recovery programme was started which led to all-round growth in Ghana. Gradually, Ghana moved towards total democracy, a process that was completed by the 1991 elections.

    Nigeria
        This country has huge oil reserves and had a huge advantage over its other continental neighbours. But it had the problem of political instability. It has been in the grip of a ‘Coup Culture’ which sees periodic dismissals of democratic governments by factions of the country’s military. The military leaders, who Stage these coups, themselves fall prey to the next conspiracy.

        Between 1960 and 1966 all went well under Prime Minister Abubakar Tafawa Balewa. But in 1966 a military coup overthrew his liberal government and civil war broke out. This was primarily due to serious tribal conflicts. In the north, the Muslims vied for supremacy with the Hausa and Fulani tribes. In the west, the Yorubas, and in the east, the Ibos harboured the grievance that the more powerful northern region, from where Balewa himself hailed, was dominating them. Thus, the Ibo officers in the Army Staged a coup. Balewa and many leading politicians were killed. This sparked off ethnic strife in the country. General Ironsi, who made himself the new leader, was himself killed by soldiers hailing from the north. They installed Colonel Yakubu Gowon in ; power which led to large scale migration of the Ibos from other parts of the country to the east. In 1967, under Colonel Ojukwu, they declared themselves independent of Nigeria and called their new country Biafra,

        The civil war between Lagos and the east raged for three years. Thousands of people in the east were killed, but more due to starvation than warfare. Neither the UN nor the Commonwealth could bring peace before, literally the last Biafra soldier, finished fighting. After the civil war was over, Colonel ' Gowon restructured the country’s administration by setting up a new federal system consisting of 12 states (later increased to 19) to give more representation to every tribal group. The mid-1970s oil scare which made oil exporting countries rich benefited Nigeria too. The country’s economic condition improved though political instability remained a problem. In 1975, the Army, which did not like the way Gowon was returning the country to civilian rule, was upstaged in a coup. But under international pressure, elections were held in 1979 in which a civilian government under President Shagari caine to power.

        In the 1980s, Nigeria’s economy, which was over-reliant on oil prices, collapsed because of a recession in global trade. In August 1983, Shagari fell to another coup when Major General Bukhari made himself President. But Bukhari himself did not last long. Frustrated over the deteriorating economic conditions, a rival group of Army officers led by Major General Babangida came to power. He emulated Rawlings’ example and ruled through a democratic structure made up of eminent civilians. Though he tried to . diversify the Nigerian industrial base, yet the constantly falling oil prices in the world market undermined his efforts. For the first time, in 1986, Nigeria was forced to accept a World Bank loan to sustain its economic recovery programme. The road to the return of democracy was also smoothened. But once again the ‘coup culture’ intervened. In 1993, General Sani Abacha staged a bloodless coup and began a highly authoritarian rule. Several important politicians and intellectuals, including the famous novelist Ken Saro Wiwa, was put in prison and then hanged on flimsy charges. This attracted global conde- mnation and Nigeria found itself isolated in the world. Sanctions were imposed on it, preventing it from selling its oil. In the north, the Muslims imposed the rule of the SHariat which led to widespread human rights abuses. Thus, the once proud nation with considerable potential to match South Africa as an economic power, slipped to the status of an international pariah state.

    Angola
        A bitter civil war broke out in Angola soon after it became independent of the Portuguese colonial rule in 1975. There were three different liberation struggles without any link among them. Once the Portuguese left, they began to fight each other for control.

        The Popular Movement for the Liberation of Angola (MPLA) was left wing. It installed itself in power under Agostinho Neto. The National Union for the Total independence of Angola (UNITA), led by Jonas Savimbi, had its support base among the Ovimbundu tribes of the country’s south. The National Front for the Liberation’ of Angola (FNLA), though weaker than the MPLA and UNITA, had pockets of support, particularly among the Bakongo tribe in the country’s north- west. The rise to power of the MPLA alarmed the western nations as it had a communist character about it. The United States, along with President Mobutu of neighbouring Zaire, backed the UNITA with arms and ammunition. ‘Cuba decided to support the MPLA. South Africa, which was against the MPLA for backing the South West African People’s Organisation (SWAPO) of Namibia (a country illegally held by South Africa) invaded the country from the south. Zairean forces stormed the country’s north-east. This complicated the situation. 

        The civil war dragged on over a decade. Finally, in December 1988, the UN brokered a peace arrangement under which South Africa agreed to give independence to Namibia. The Cuban troops left Angola. Under Sam Nujoma, thus, Namibia became a democratic republic in 1990 and elections were held in 1992 under UN supervision which returned Nujoma’s MPAA to power. But by this time world communism was tottering and support from Moscow and the East’ Bloc ceased. This emboldened the UNITA to make one more attempt to seize power. Jonas Savimbi, its leader, refused to accept the results of the elections even though they were conducted under the watchful eyes of the UN. The fighting raged for six months until the US, which had so ‘far withheld recognition to the MPAA, formally recognised the Nujoma government. In 1993, the UNITA rebels were brought back to the negotiating table. It accepted the results of the 1992 elections and agreed to join a coalition government with the MPAA.

    Burundi
        There was tension between Burundi and Rwanda, the two former Belgian countries right from the time they gained their independence in 1962. The Tutsi tribe, which was in a minority, held power in Burundi. It was resented by the Hutus. Therefore, in 1988, the Hutu tribal soldiers in the Burundi Army turned against the Tutsis and massacred thousands of them. When the country’s first democratic elections were held, in 1993, the Hutus emerged victorious. But the Tutsis resented this and murdered the new President. Then began a cycle of killings and reprisal killings, throwing the country into complete chaos. Fighting continued until 1996. Alarmed by the endless bloodbath, the Organisation of African Unity sent a peace-keeping force to the country in 1996. Burundi was under an Army-backed government in which the Prime Minister was a Tutsi and the President a Hutu. But when even the peace-keeping force could not bring peace, the Army dismissed the government and seized power under Major Pierre Buyoya, a Tutsi.

    Rwanda
        Here too, there was intense Tutsi-Hutu rivalry which resulted in frequent incidents of genocide. In 1990, fighting broke out between the Tutsi- dominated Rwandese Patriotic Front (RPF) and the Rwandan Army in which the Hutus called the shots. The UN sent a peace-keeping force while international mediation went on to end the civil war. There was temporary peace from October 1993. But, in early 1994, an unusual assassination was carried out. The Presidents of Rwanda and Burundi, both Hutus, held a meeting in Tanzania and were flying in the same aircraft back to Rwanda’s capital, Kigali. Shortly before landing, it was brought down by a missile, killing all on board. This let loose a horrible slaughter in African history. Thousands of Tutsi men, women and children were massacred. Though there was a UN peace-keeping force stationed in the country, it could do little to prevent the killings. More than half a million Tutsis were killed over the next few months and more than a million of them fled to neighbouring Zaire and Tanzania.This endless massacre shocked the world. Despite repeated UN missions of peace and attempts by Hutu moderates, the cycle of violence has not ended.

    Zaire (formerly Belgian Congo)
        This country had over 150 tribes, While departing, in 1960, the Belgian ' colonists handed over power to a coalition government. But fighting broke out soon, There was also a rebellion in the Army, still controlled by Belgian officers. The Belgian copper mining company, Union Miniere, which controlled the rich copper areas of the south-eastern province of Katanga, sponsored a secessionist movement which encouraged the local tribes to announce the formation of a separate country called Moise Tshombe. The UN stepped in to intervene. The UN Secretary General, Dag Hammarskjold, was suspected by many observers as playing partisan role in this affair. President Patrice Lumumba appealed to the world body to send a peace- keeping force. Though the UN stationed about 3,000 troops in the country, Hammarskjold yielded to the Belgian pressure in favour of an Independent Katanga which would have suited the former colonial power to continue to exploit the region's copper. Though the UN did not recognise Katanga, yet it did nothing to prevent it from functioning as a free state. Lumumba appealed to Moscow for help. This brought the Americans into the picture. Abetted by them President Joseph Kasavubu imprisoned Lumumba and then killed him. After Hammarskjold died'in an air crash, his successor, U. Thant, a Burmese, decided to put an end to the UN’s duplicity. In September 1962, the UN troops intervened in Katanga and reunified the country.

        The Katanga conflict was only part of the young nation’s problems. Tribal rivalries frequently led to armed clashes. In 1965, General Joseph Mobutu, used foreign mercenaries to seize power. He provided Congo with an authoritarian government and strong central authority which gradually helped the country return to normalcy. But in the late 1970s, Zaire ( the nation’s new name) was invaded by Angolan troops who took over Katanga (renamed Shaba). Though that problem was staved off thanks to international intervention, Zaire’s economic woes multiplied because of a decline in world copper price which made food imports costly. Mobutu's authoritarianism assumed a brutal nature as civic unrest grew. In 1990, Mobutu, under international pressure, lifted the ban on multiparty politics.

        In 1994, the ethnic massacres in neighbouring Rwanda led to hundreds of thousands of Tutsis cross over into eastern Zaire where the clashes Zairean Army intervened and drove back the refugees into Rwanda where they faced the prospect of extermination by the Hutus. Meanwhile, Mobutu’s health deteriorated and he was hospitalised in Europe for extended periods. General Laurence Kabila, with the help of the Rwandan government, rebelled in the eastern part of the country. The Zairean Army put up little resistance as Kabila grabbed more and more territory to the west. In May 1997, his troops entered the capital, Kinshasa. Mobutu was forced into exile. The country was renamed ‘Democratic Republic of Congo’. 

        Kabila could not restore calm in the country. Rebels assisted by Uganda and Rwanda staged an uprising and raided Kinshasa in August 1998. Kabila got aid from Angola, Namibia and Zimbabwe in beating back the raiders. A cease-fire was declared in August 1999. Kabila was murdered by one of his own bodyguards and was succeeded by his son Joseph.

        Incidentally, there is another country called the Republic of Congo in western Africa which is also known as Congo-Brazaville. It gained its independence from France in 1960.

    Tanzania
        This is one of the poorest countries of Africa, but tribalism is not a problem. The Swahili language acts as a common bond. President Julius Kambarage Nyerere’s visionary leadership, based on socialist principles contained in his famous ‘Arusha Declaration’ led to the diversification of the country's economic base. The Declaration emphasised that the state must have effective control over all means of production and prevent the exploitation of the poor by the rich. He strengthened the country’s agricultural base. Though there was a single party rule, genuine democracy existed. He offered the developing world an alternative to western models of growth and governance. The other African countries blindly copied the western example and suffered civil war, ethnic strife, vast disparities in living standards between the rich and the poor. Nyerere was an outspoken critic of the world economy and the way it had reduced the majority of the nations into poverty, He played an important role in smashing the rogue regime of General Idi Amin in neighbouring Uganda and was a prime mover of the Organisation of African Unity. Nyerere retired in 1986. In 1992, the Constitution was amended to establish a multi-party system and privatisation was allowed gradually. Nyerere died in 1999. 

    South Africa
        We now come to the rich, most industrious but equally controversial country of the continent - South Africa. Till the 1990s, this country was a byword for everything that was retrogressive and anti-humanist on the same levels as the Nazi Germany and the Stalinist USSR for following apartheid, a policy based on racial discrimination,

        Seventy per cent of this country’s population was constituted of Blacks and another 12 per cent of other non- White communities, including ethnic Indians and other Asians. But the Whites, who comprised only 18 per cent of the population dominated every institution in the country, be it political or social, and kept others in reserved areas away from where they lived. The total land area of the reserved Black quarters was only about seven per cent of the country. Blacks were forbidden from entering into non-reserved areas unless they carried passes to prove that they worked in the other parts of the country where the Whites lived in opulence and comfort.

        A more stringent form of apartheid, meaning racial segregation or seperateniess, was introduced by Daniel Malan, the Prime Minister of South Africa in 1948-54. The Afrikaner Nationalist Party which Malan headed, got wide support from the country’s Whites who feared an end to their domination of South Africa as a result of India attaining its independence and the British Commonwealth upholding the spirit of racial equality. Nazi style propaganda espousing superiority of the White people over non-Whites was spread. Surprisingly, the Dutch Reformed Church, the official Church of South Africa, supported this view. Malan’s successors, Strijdom (1 954-58) and Vorster (1966-78) continued with apartheid despite international condemnation that reduced South Africa to the status of a pariah nation.

        Apartheid took disgusting appearances. The non-Whites were forced to live in crowded townships where sanitation was practically absent. There was nothing permanent about their dwelling. They could be uprooted from their shanty towns if a local White community decided that the non-Whites lived too close for their comfort. There were Separate buses, trains, public toilets, even park benches for the non-Whites. Their children went to inferior schools. Every person had to carry a racial identity card and could be challenged by a White policeman at any time to prove that he was in an unreserved area on work. They had no political rights and the country’s Parliament was to tally White dominated.

        Anybody who opposed this system was branded a communist and could be held in custody indefinitely under the Suppression of Communism Act. In Spite of this, the African National Congress, dared ‘to start a protest movement. In 1952, its leader, Albert Luthuli, organised a Satyagraha- type movement (the ANC openly acknowledged Mahatma Gandhi as its inspiration) by stopping work on certain days. Its workers walked into shops reserved for Whites. The government cracked down on this ina barbarous fashion. Over 8,000 people were flogged in public. Luthuli was jailed and the ANC was suppressed. But the other non-White groups like the Asians followed suit. In 1955, the ANC formed a coalition with these outfits and developed a ‘Freedom Charter’ which proclaimed that “South Africa belongs to all who live in it, Black and White, and no government can claim authority unless it is based on the will of the people.” It demanded equality before the law, freedom of assembly, movement, speech, religion and the press, the right to vote, work for equal pay, a 40-hour working week with unemployment benefits, free medical care and free and equal education.

        The cycle of protests and reprisals reached a crescendo. The most brutal government crackdown was witnessed at Sharpville in the outskirts of Johannesburg in 1960 when a peaceful demonstration against the hated Pass Law was fired upon by the police. Sixty-seven people were killed and hundreds wounded. This convinced many Black leaders that non-violence was not working and soon bomb. attacks were launched. This prompted large-scale persecution. The -ANC was banned and Nelson Mandela, its most charismatic leader was arrested and sentenced to life imprisonment. The ANC, however, carried-on protesting. Its leader, Luthuli, died in 1967. But it is widely believed that he was murdered,

        International Opposition to Apartheid
            Apartheid was considered an anachronism in the 20th century. South Africa was about to be expelled from the Commonwealth, but its government under Verwoerd avoided the humiliation by withdrawing its membership and declaring the country a Republic. The United Nations voted in favour of imposing an economic boycott of South Africa. However, it could not work well because many of its member states, including the US, Britain, France, West Germany and Italy continued to trade with it. But they condemned apartheid in public. During the Cold War years, the western countries even supplied arms to the country believing it could be used as a force against the spread of communism in the African continent.

            Black African protest movements continued despite severe government crackdowns. In 1976, there was another massacre, this time in Soweto, a Black township near Johannesburg, in which 200 people were killed. Steve Biko, another popular young leader, was beaten to death by the police.

        Rollback of Apartheid
            In 1979, the government in South Africa under Prime Minister P.W. (‘Pic’) Botha, decided to reform the racist laws as South Africa was surrounded by hostile countries which refused to trade with it. This caused serious economic problems for the country. The White population decreased to 16 per cent because of large-scale emigration while the Black population went up. In the 1950s, the racist government had promoted Independent tribal ‘homeland’ states in the country, but controlled almost all their affairs, This experiment failed because the rulers of these ‘homeland’ states, co-opted by the racist regime, were corrupt and their people were mired in deep poverty.
